(2020) show that BERT pruned during pretraining transfers to downstream tasks, so the structural step need not wait for a task-specific model.

These operational gains trade inference efficiency for substantial training overhead. Distillation requires running inference across the teacher model to generate soft targets and backpropagating loss through the student across millions of tokens. For large models, this auxiliary training phase consumes significant accelerator FLOPs and memory bandwidth, whereas post-training quantization, introduced later in this chapter, needs only a pass over a small calibration set. Compression effectiveness is also bounded by student capacity and teacher fidelity. An undersized student lacks the capacity to absorb the teacher’s distribution, while an inaccurate or uncalibrated teacher imparts its own systematic errors to the student. Selecting an optimization strategy therefore requires evaluating task accuracy, parameter footprint, serving latency, and total training cost together, the comparison that table 11 in section 10.5 makes for distillation, pruning, and quantization.

Distillation still needs a student architecture, and the student’s own design decides how much of the frame budget remains for everything else. Efficient architecture design and neural architecture search build that student to be efficient on the target by construction.

10.2.4 Efficient architecture design

Hardware-aware design begins before compression. If the target device cannot keep convolution kernels fed, cannot store activations without spilling, or cannot meet the power budget at the chosen input resolution, pruning and quantization only treat symptoms. The architecture itself must expose the kind of work the hardware can execute efficiently. That means choosing layer shapes, scaling rules, and operator patterns with memory bandwidth, parallelism, and energy as first-class constraints rather than post-hoc deployment checks. The efficient families of Network Architectures, among them MobileNetV2’s depthwise separable factorization, are best read here as responses to a measured deployment bottleneck rather than as a catalog of architectures.

Efficient design principles

A camera pipeline that misses its 33 ms frame budget because \(3{\times}3\) convolutions saturate the mobile NPU needs a different architectural rewrite from a keyword spotter whose weights must fit inside 512 KB of microcontroller SRAM. Identifying which resource binds in the profiling trace is therefore the first design step: a model can miss its target because every stage is scaled too large, because one convolution family dominates arithmetic, because activations or parameters overflow the memory hierarchy, or because the candidate graph uses operators the platform executes poorly. Table 5 organizes the common architectural responses by the bottleneck they address rather than by model family.

These responses interact. Reducing convolutional FLOPs with depthwise separable convolutions helps only if the target runtime has efficient kernels for the resulting operators. Shrinking a model with parameter-reduction layers helps only if activation storage or memory traffic was part of the measured problem. Hardware-aware design therefore does not replace profiling; it moves profiling information earlier, into the architecture itself.

Scaling optimization

The first architectural response is global. When every stage of the profile is over budget, the problem is not one bad layer; the model is scaled incorrectly for the deployment envelope. The design task is then to distribute capacity across depth, width, and input resolution rather than choose a single parameter count. Depth adds sequential stages and activation storage. Width exposes more parallel work within each stage at the cost of larger weight and activation tensors. Higher input resolution sharpens spatial detail by multiplying the positions every convolution must visit. Which dimension to grow therefore depends on the machine, since a wide parallel accelerator can absorb extra channels while a small edge device is limited first by memory capacity and energy per access.

Mathematically, the total FLOPs for a convolutional model can be approximated as: \[ \Operations\propto \LayerCount \cdot \WidthMultiplier^2 \cdot \InputResolution^2, \] where \(\LayerCount\) is depth (number of layers), \(\WidthMultiplier\) is the width multiplier (channel count relative to a base model), and \(\InputResolution\) is the input resolution. This expression shows why naive scaling fails. Increasing width and resolution together multiplies work quickly, and the resulting model may exceed the memory bandwidth or power budget even when the parameter count appears reasonable.

The camera pipeline’s own architecture exposes one of these knobs directly. MobileNetV2’s width multiplier scales every layer’s channel count, and at a width of 0.75 the network shrinks from 3.5M to 2.6M parameters and from 600 MFLOP to 418 MFLOP per frame, keeping about 70 percent of the arithmetic. The square law predicts a larger cut, because depthwise layers cost in proportion to the width rather than its square, and the network’s final convolution keeps its full width at reduced multipliers. A width setting is therefore a trade the phone can measure directly, in milliseconds per frame, against the accuracy the narrower network loses.

Compound scaling turns this balancing act into a controlled design rule. Instead of adjusting depth, width, and resolution independently, compound scaling grows all three dimensions by fixed ratios \((\DepthScalingRatio, \WidthScalingRatio, \ResolutionScalingRatio)\) relative to a base model: \[ \LayerCount = \DepthScalingRatio^\CompoundScalingCoefficient \BaseLayerCount, \quad \WidthMultiplier= \WidthScalingRatio^\CompoundScalingCoefficient \BaseWidthMultiplier, \quad \InputResolution= \ResolutionScalingRatio^\CompoundScalingCoefficient \InputResolution_0 \] Here, \(\CompoundScalingCoefficient\) is a scaling coefficient, and \(\DepthScalingRatio\), \(\WidthScalingRatio\), and \(\ResolutionScalingRatio\) (as in the original EfficientNet paper) are scaling factors determined from empirical accuracy and efficiency measurements. The rule matters because it prevents one dimension from consuming the budget before the others can contribute useful accuracy.

EfficientNet (section 10.2.5) validated this principle by using search to find a baseline architecture and then scaling it with balanced depth, width, and resolution coefficients (Tan and Le 2019). The lesson is not that every deployment should use EfficientNet. The systems lesson is that scaling is a resource-allocation decision, because the same accuracy target can imply different depth-width-resolution trade-offs depending on which resource the target platform makes scarce. Benchmarking formalizes how to measure those trade-offs; the design principle here is to scale the dimensions against the binding resource.

The same logic extends beyond convolutional models. Transformer layers, attention heads, sequence length, and embedding width play roles analogous to depth, width, and resolution. Each increases capacity, but each stresses compute, memory bandwidth, or activation storage differently. Hardware-aware scaling keeps those dimensions tied to the measured bottleneck instead of treating model size as a single scalar.

Computation reduction

If the profile shows that a small set of convolutional operators dominates arithmetic, reducing the whole model uniformly is wasteful. The better response is to change the expensive operator. Modern efficient architectures do this by factorizing dense computations into cheaper pieces that preserve the representation needed for accuracy.

Depthwise separable convolutions, the factorization behind the MobileNetV2 and KWS lighthouses (Howard et al. 2017; Sandler et al. 2018), are the standard example. They split a standard convolution into a depthwise stage that filters each input channel separately and a pointwise \(1{\times}1\) stage that mixes channels. This cuts the arithmetic to about \(1/\LayerOutputWidth + 1/\KernelSize^2\) of a standard layer with \(\LayerOutputWidth\) output channels and kernel width \(\KernelSize\), roughly one-ninth for \(3{\times}3\) kernels and many output channels. What this chapter adds to that result is the deployment condition. The wall-clock benefit depends on kernel support and memory behavior. A mobile runtime with optimized depthwise kernels can convert much of this arithmetic reduction into latency savings, while a poorly supported backend may expose the factorized operations as many small, memory-bound kernels.

Howard, Andrew G., Menglong Zhu, Bo Chen, Dmitry Kalenichenko, Weijun Wang, Tobias Weyand, Marco Andreetto, and Hartwig Adam. 2017. “MobileNets: Efficient Convolutional Neural Networks for Mobile Vision Applications.” CoRR abs/1704.04861.

↩︎ Depthwise separable convolution and its cost reduction relative to a standard convolution are established in Depthwise separable convolutions.

Other factorization patterns follow the same logic. ResNeXt’s grouped convolutions split feature maps into independent channel groups before merging them, and ResNet’s bottleneck blocks use \(1{\times}1\) projections to narrow the channel dimension before an expensive \(3{\times}3\) spatial convolution and restore it afterward. Because all of these rewrites help only when the spatial convolution they shrink actually dominates the trace, they yield much smaller gains once memory traffic, launch overhead, or unsupported kernels become the binding cost.

Arithmetic reduction is only one possible response. When profiling instead identifies memory capacity or data movement as the binding resource on the target device, the architecture must address that memory bottleneck directly.

Memory optimization

When the profile points to memory rather than arithmetic, the architecture must reduce the working set or the number of expensive memory accesses. Activations, feature maps, and parameters can exceed cache, SRAM, accelerator memory, or edge-device storage even when the FLOP count is acceptable. Memory-efficient architectures therefore try to preserve useful information while storing or moving less data.

DenseNet illustrates the feature-reuse response (Huang et al. 2017). In a traditional convolutional network, each layer computes a new set of feature maps, increasing the activation footprint as the network deepens. DenseNet connects layers so later computations can reuse earlier feature maps instead of relearning similar representations. In DenseNet with \(\LayerCount\) layers and growth rate \(\FeatureMapGrowthRate\), each layer generates \(\FeatureMapGrowthRate\) new feature maps concatenated with prior layers, causing the total number of feature maps to grow linearly: \[ \mathcal{O}(\LayerCount \FeatureMapGrowthRate) \]

Huang, Gao, Zhuang Liu, Laurens Van Der Maaten, and Kilian Q. Weinberger. 2017. “Densely Connected Convolutional Networks.” 2017 IEEE Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition (CVPR), 2261–69. https://doi.org/10.1109/cvpr.2017.243.

DenseNet reduces parameter redundancy through feature reuse, but concatenating retained features can increase activation storage and memory traffic. The resulting working set and runtime must be measured on the target hardware.

Parameter reduction applies the channel-narrowing idea of a \(1{\times}1\) projection directly to weight storage. SqueezeNet’s fire modules replace \(3{\times}3\) filters with a \(1{\times}1\) “squeeze” layer that contracts the input channel count \(\LayerInputWidth\) before feeding a mixed \(1{\times}1\) and \(3{\times}3\) “expand” layer (Iandola et al. 2016). Because the parameter count of a standard convolutional layer scales with the product of both channel dimensions and the spatial kernel area, \[ \mathcal{O}(\LayerInputWidth \LayerOutputWidth \KernelSize^2) \] shrinking \(\LayerInputWidth\) before the \(3{\times}3\) filters cuts stored weights sharply, achieving the paper’s AlexNet-level accuracy target with far fewer parameters than AlexNet. That trade is attractive when the deployment constraint is flash storage, model download size, or parameter bandwidth; it is less decisive when activation memory or operator overhead dominates.

Iandola, Forrest N., Song Han, Matthew W. Moskewicz, Khalid Ashraf, William J. Dally, and Kurt Keutzer. 2016. “SqueezeNet: AlexNet-Level Accuracy with 50x Fewer Parameters and <0.5MB Model Size.” ArXiv Preprint abs/1602.07360.

Feature reuse and parameter reduction are therefore not interchangeable recipes: reused features cut redundant representations, whereas \(1{\times}1\) bottlenecks cut parameter movement at deployment, so the profile’s binding memory term decides between them. Each of these manual motifs fixes one operator pattern at a time, leaving open how to choose kernel sizes, expansion ratios, and channel widths jointly across an entire network.

10.2.5 Neural architecture search

Hand-tuning kernel sizes, expansion ratios, and channel widths across twenty or more layers can explore only a handful of the billions of valid configurations that fit a phone’s latency budget, leaving significant accuracy or speed on the table. Neural architecture search (NAS) automates this structural design by systematically traversing a bounded space of operations, topologies, and compound scaling rules prior to training (Elsken et al. 2019). Early reinforcement-learning formulations maximized task accuracy without hardware constraints (Zoph and Le 2016); subsequent weight-sharing methods amortized evaluation overhead (Pham et al. 2018); and hardware-aware NAS explicitly incorporated measured device latency and energy into the multi-objective search (Tan et al. 2019). For the camera pipeline, architecture search is already part of the model’s lineage, since MnasNet and MobileNetV3, MobileNetV2’s successors, came from searches that measured candidate latency on phones (Tan et al. 2019; Howard et al. 2019).

Elsken, Thomas, Jan Hendrik Metzen, and Frank Hutter. 2019. “Neural Architecture Search.” In The Springer Series on Challenges in Machine Learning, vol. 20. Springer International Publishing. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-05318-5_3.

12 Hardware-aware NAS: Optimizes measured latency rather than FLOPs, which can diverge by 3–5\(\times\) when memory access or operator support dominates. Tan et al. (2019) fed device latency into search and found architectures 1.8\(\times\) faster than MobileNetV2 at higher accuracy.

Tan, Mingxing, Bo Chen, Ruoming Pang, Vijay Vasudevan, Mark Sandler, Andrew Howard, and Quoc V. Le. 2019. “MnasNet: Platform-Aware Neural Architecture Search for Mobile.” 2019 IEEE/CVF Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition (CVPR), 2815–23. https://doi.org/10.1109/cvpr.2019.00293.

Discovery runs as a closed loop. The search strategy samples candidate architectures \(\CandidateArchitecture\in \ArchitectureSearchSpace\) from the defined search space, and the performance estimation engine evaluates their latency or accuracy and feeds the result back to refine subsequent proposals (figure 13). NAS12 returns graphs that sit on the Pareto frontier under explicit resource bounds.

The NAS optimization problem (optional depth)

NAS rests on three design decisions: the search space, the search strategy, and how each candidate is evaluated. An architecture’s quality cannot be known until it is trained, and training is expensive, which creates two nested decisions: which operations to include (the architecture) and which parameters work best for them (the weights).

NAS is therefore a bi-level optimization problem.13 The outer loop searches the architecture space \(\ArchitectureSearchSpace\), while the inner loop trains candidate architectures to evaluate performance. Formally, the objective seeks the optimal architecture \(\CandidateArchitecture^*\) that minimizes validation loss \(\ValidationLoss\) under constraints \(\ArchitectureResourceCost\) (latency, memory): \[ \CandidateArchitecture^* = \operatorname{arg\,min}_{\CandidateArchitecture\in \ArchitectureSearchSpace} \ValidationLoss(\ModelParameters^*(\CandidateArchitecture), \CandidateArchitecture) \quad \text{subject to} \quad \ArchitectureResourceCost(\CandidateArchitecture) \leq \ResourceCostBudget\] where \(\ModelParameters^*(\CandidateArchitecture)\) represents the optimal model parameters for architecture \(\CandidateArchitecture\), obtained by minimizing training loss: \[ \ModelParameters^*(\CandidateArchitecture) = \operatorname{arg\,min}_{\ModelParameters} \TrainingLoss(\ModelParameters, \CandidateArchitecture) \]

13 Bi-level optimization: A formulation where one optimization problem sits inside another. In NAS, the outer level selects an architecture while the inner level trains that candidate’s weights, so early methods paid a full training cost for every architecture evaluated. Weight-sharing methods such as Efficient Neural Architecture Search (ENAS) amortize one training run across many candidates, reducing search cost by roughly 1,000× (Pham et al. 2018).

Pham, Hieu, Melody Y. Guan, Barret Zoph, Quoc V. Le, and Jeff Dean. 2018. “Efficient Neural Architecture Search via Parameter Sharing.” Proceedings of the 35th International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML), Proceedings of machine learning research, vol. 80: 4095–104.

The computational bottleneck lies in this inner loop. If evaluating every candidate requires independent training from scratch, a modest search space of 10 operation choices across 20 layers yields \(10^{20}\) possible architectures, making exhaustive enumeration impossible. Practical NAS methods overcome this combinatorial explosion through three mechanisms: restricting the search space to reusable cells, amortizing evaluation through weight sharing, or relaxing discrete choices into continuous, differentiable formulations.

Search space design (optional depth)

Many NAS systems search for reusable cells, small directed acyclic graphs of operations that are stacked to form complete networks, rather than searching every layer of a deep backbone independently. Within a cell, each node represents an intermediate feature map and each directed edge applies a candidate primitive chosen from \(3{\times}3\) convolution, \(5{\times}5\) convolution, depthwise separable convolution, max pooling, or an identity skip connection. Because the search algorithm only has to wire up this local graph, a simplified cell with four nodes and two candidate operations per edge yields roughly 10,000 possible cell designs, decoupling the size of the search space from the final depth of the network. NASNet established this pattern by searching for two complementary motifs: a normal cell that preserves spatial height, width, and channel count when stacked repeatedly, and a reduction cell that uses stride-two operations to halve spatial resolution and double the channel count at stage boundaries. Once discovered on a small, fast proxy task, the same normal and reduction cells can be stacked deeper or wider to match the compute budget of the deployment target.

Search spaces also encode hardware constraints by restricting candidate operations to primitives with efficient kernels on the target platform. Mobile search spaces, for example, prioritize depthwise separable convolutions with bounded kernel sizes and channel counts (Zhang et al. 2020). MobileNetV3 paired this structured block-level search space with NetAdapt to prune layer channels against measured mobile CPU latency (Howard et al. 2019).

Zhang, Li Lyna, Yuqing Yang, Yuhang Jiang, Wenwu Zhu, and Yunxin Liu. 2020. “Fast Hardware-Aware Neural Architecture Search.” 2020 IEEE/CVF Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition Workshops (CVPRW). https://doi.org/10.1109/cvprw50498.2020.00354.

Search strategies (optional depth)

Search strategies trade search cost against architectural diversity (table 6). Reinforcement learning treats search as a sequential decision in which a controller proposes architectures and receives an accuracy reward. It discovered NASNet, but every reward requires training a candidate. The search of Zoph and Le (2016) evaluated roughly 12,800 architectures on 800 GPUs for 28 days, a total of 22,400 GPU-days, a cost that pushed practical NAS toward weight sharing and cheaper performance predictors. Evolutionary algorithms instead mutate and recombine a population of candidates and keep the fittest. AmoebaNet reached ImageNet accuracy competitive with top human-designed models after 3,150 GPU-days (Real et al. 2019), a cost that needs proxy tasks, weight sharing, or extensive parallelism to control. Gradient-based methods such as DARTS (Differentiable Architecture Search) (Liu et al. 2019) relax the search space into weighted combinations of all operations. They optimize architecture weights and model weights jointly by gradient descent, which reduces search cost from hundreds of GPU-days to one to four, though the relaxation may miss discrete patterns that discrete search finds.

Hardware-aware NAS optimizes execution metrics on target silicon rather than FLOP counts, which correlate poorly with latency when memory bandwidth or launch overheads dominate. Under the arithmetic intensity law (principle 6), an operation with few FLOPs but low arithmetic intensity can run slower than a higher-FLOP operation that saturates compute. MnasNet addresses this divergence by measuring each candidate’s latency directly on a mobile phone and folding it into the reward, which combines accuracy and latency through a weighted product: \[ \text{Reward}(\CandidateArchitecture) = \text{Accuracy}(\CandidateArchitecture) \times \left(\frac{\TargetInferenceLatency}{\InferenceLatency(\CandidateArchitecture)}\right)^{\LatencyRewardExponent}\] where \(\InferenceLatency(\CandidateArchitecture)\) is the measured on-device inference latency of architecture \(\CandidateArchitecture\), \(\TargetInferenceLatency\) is the latency constraint, and the exponent \(\LatencyRewardExponent\) controls the accuracy-latency trade-off. Architectures that exceed the latency target are penalized. In the reported MnasNet search space, varying inverted-residual expansion ratios produced a stronger accuracy-latency trade-off than uniform expansion.

When to use NAS

Neural architecture search can discover architectures that outperform hand-designed alternatives, but its cost demands a check on whether the investment is justified. NAS can be worthwhile for novel hardware platforms with unusual constraints, such as new accelerator architectures or highly constrained edge devices, where existing architectures are poorly optimized. It can also make sense at deployment scales where small efficiency improvements justify the upfront search cost, or when architecture families for cloud, edge, and mobile deployments can amortize one search across many variants.

Conversely, custom NAS is difficult to justify when standard deployment constraints already have well-optimized architecture families. If the compute budget is only a few GPU-days, large reinforcement-learning or evolutionary searches are impractical; differentiable or weight-sharing methods such as DARTS may remain feasible, but still require deployment-scale and validation-cost justification. Rapidly changing requirements also weaken the case because the target can move before search and validation finish.

For most practitioners, existing NAS-discovered or NAS-assisted architectures provide strong baselines before funding a search from scratch. EfficientNet jointly scales depth, width, and resolution with compound coefficients (Tan and Le 2019), MobileNetV3 combines hardware-aware search with NetAdapt for phone latency targets (Howard et al. 2019), MnasNet folds measured phone latency into its reward (Tan et al. 2019), and FBNet puts device-specific latency into a mobile-CPU search objective (Wu et al. 2019). NAS has also been applied to compact transformer models under compute or memory constraints. These results show what search found under particular spaces, objectives, and measurements, not that search universally beats careful manual design. When latency, memory, or energy enters the objective and is measured on the target platform, the search evaluates candidates against the resource deployment actually constrains, yet the transfer quality of the resulting architectures still depends on task and hardware. Reserve custom NAS for unusual constraints or deployment scales that can amortize the search and validation investment. Even a searched architecture, however, still executes the same fixed graph on every input, spending as much compute on a blank background frame as on a crowded street scene.

Howard, Andrew, Mark Sandler, Bo Chen, Weijun Wang, Liang-Chieh Chen, Mingxing Tan, Grace Chu, et al. 2019. “Searching for MobileNetV3.” 2019 IEEE/CVF International Conference on Computer Vision (ICCV), 1314–24. https://doi.org/10.1109/iccv.2019.00140.
Wu, Bichen, Kurt Keutzer, Xiaoliang Dai, Peizhao Zhang, Yanghan Wang, Fei Sun, Yiming Wu, Yuandong Tian, Peter Vajda, and Yangqing Jia. 2019. “FBNet: Hardware-Aware Efficient ConvNet Design via Differentiable Neural Architecture Search.” 2019 IEEE/CVF Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition (CVPR), 10726–34. https://doi.org/10.1109/cvpr.2019.01099.

10.2.6 Adaptive computation methods

In the phone’s camera pipeline, a static backbone runs the same multiply-accumulate (MAC) operations on a blank background frame as on a crowded street scene, just as a static 24-layer transformer streams its entire parameter footprint from HBM for an obvious token as for an ambiguous one. Because pruning, distillation, factorization, and architecture search all produce a fixed execution graph, every input follows the same scheduled path regardless of difficulty. Adaptive inference replaces this open-loop pipeline with an input-dependent control loop in which intermediate representations or runtime budgets decide whether to terminate execution early or which specialized subnetwork receives the activations. While dynamic paths can reduce average arithmetic operations and memory traffic, they introduce runtime overheads in routing logic, batch management, confidence calibration, and latency variance. Single-stream edge workloads such as the camera pipeline are natural candidates because many frames are easy and the phone processes one frame at a time. Dynamic pruning applies conditional execution at the fine granularity of individual channels and filters; coarser dynamic schemes skip whole layers, exit early, or route tokens across expert subnetworks.

Dynamic pruning

Traditional pruning methods, whether unstructured or structured, produce a static pruning mask or subnetwork that remains fixed during deployment, even if the mask was learned gradually during training. Dynamic pruning instead decides at inference time, from each input, which computation is active, so the executed subnetwork changes from one input to the next.

Dynamic pruning can therefore allocate different amounts of work to different inputs and can use, for a hard input, capacity that a static deployment mask would remove permanently. Activation-conditioned pruning, for example, deactivates neurons or channels for particular inputs (Hu et al. 2023). On a simple image with mostly uniform regions, some convolutional filters produce negligible activations, and a dynamic policy can exclude them for that frame. The exclusion reduces nominal work, but the controller sits on the critical path, input-dependent routing fragments execution across SIMD vector lanes, and any quality change remains part of the result. Training may also need routing losses or regularization so that the policy actually uses its cheaper paths. Dynamic pruning is therefore a candidate when input complexity varies enough to repay the controller and batching overhead, not a default substitute for static pruning, and its gain must be measured as latency and accuracy together across the full input distribution.

Hu, Jie, Peng Lin, Huajun Zhang, Zining Lan, Wenxin Chen, Kailiang Xie, Siyun Chen, Hao Wang, and Sheng Chang. 2023. “A Dynamic Pruning Method on Multiple Sparse Structures in Deep Neural Networks.” IEEE Access 11: 38448–57. https://doi.org/10.1109/access.2023.3267469.

↪ Joint evaluation of compressed models on accuracy and target efficiency metrics such as latency, plotted as an efficiency-quality frontier, is measured in Compression validation: The efficiency-quality frontier.

Methods that change the mask during training are sometimes also called dynamic, but they solve a different problem. Gradual magnitude pruning progressively increases the fraction of pruned parameters while fine-tuning the survivors, which is the iterative schedule of section 10.2.1.5 run inside training, and dynamic sparse training additionally regrows connections that prove important later. Both end in a static mask for deployment, so this chapter reserves dynamic pruning for input-dependent execution. Skipping individual channels within a layer still executes every stage of the backbone; larger per-decision savings come from modulating the macro-graph itself.

Dynamic schemes

Coarser dynamic schemes route each input through only as much of the backbone as its difficulty warrants, preserving the full network for worst-case inputs while cutting whole stages or subnetworks on simple ones. Depending on whether the controller varies network depth (early exit), stage execution (conditional layer skipping), specialist capacity (mixture-of-experts), or a continuous runtime budget, each mechanism trades a larger compute saving against a coarser routing decision.

To control execution depth, early exit architectures attach auxiliary classification heads to intermediate layers, terminating execution when intermediate representations yield sufficient prediction confidence (Teerapittayanon et al. 2017). BranchyNet implements this mechanism across convolutional backbones using shallow exit branches, whereas multi-exit vision transformers attach linear probes and entropy estimators between transformer blocks (Xin et al. 2021). When an input satisfies an exit criterion, such as softmax prediction entropy dropping below a pre-tuned threshold, the runtime halts evaluation and returns the intermediate prediction. The net operational benefit requires that the arithmetic operations and memory transfers avoided in subsequent layers exceed the computational overhead of evaluating the intermediate classifiers and gating thresholds.

Teerapittayanon, Surat, Bradley McDanel, and H. T. Kung. 2017. “BranchyNet: Fast Inference via Early Exiting from Deep Neural Networks.” 2016 23rd International Conference on Pattern Recognition (ICPR), 2464–69. https://doi.org/10.1109/icpr.2016.7900006.
Hu, Ting-Kuei, Tianlong Chen, Haotao Wang, and Zhangyang Wang. 2020. “Triple Wins: Boosting Accuracy, Robustness and Efficiency Together by Enabling Input-Adaptive Inference.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).
Chen, Yanxi, Xuchen Pan, Yaliang Li, Bolin Ding, and Jingren Zhou. 2024. “EE-LLM: Large-Scale Training and Inference of Early-Exit Large Language Models with 3D Parallelism.” Proceedings of the 41st International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML).

The systems trade-off diverges sharply across hardware deployment targets. On single-sample edge processors (batch size \(\BatchSize=1\), typical of microcontrollers and mobile NPUs), early exit immediately terminates the execution thread (Hu et al. 2020). Skipping remaining layers eliminates both the arithmetic operations and the off-chip DRAM transactions required to load subsequent layer weights into on-chip SRAM cache. Conversely, on parallel throughput accelerators (GPUs and TPUs) processing batched workloads (\(\BatchSize> 1\)), early exit introduces severe batch fragmentation (Chen et al. 2024). If only a subset of samples in a batch reaches the exit threshold, the remaining samples must still traverse deeper layers. Unless the runtime executes dynamic batch compaction (gathering unresolved activation tensors into a contiguous memory buffer through memory copies and kernel synchronizations), inactive samples remain allocated in memory, or the accelerator must mask inactive SIMD or single instruction, multiple threads (SIMT) lanes. Consequently, tail latency for the batch remains dictated by the deepest traversing sample. Along a transformer backbone, each block taps its intermediate representation into an auxiliary classifier and a learned confidence estimator (learning to exit, LTE) that can trigger an early exit, while unresolved inputs continue to deeper layers (figure 14).

↪ The latency-throughput trade-offs of batching under production request streams, including service-level objective (SLO) violations, are developed in Dynamic batching latency-throughput trade-offs.

Where early exit truncates depth along a single linear pipeline, conditional computation dynamically modulates the topological routing of activations through the network graph (Bengio et al. 2015). Instead of executing every block in a predefined sequence, gating functions evaluate activation tensors to bypass residual layers, alter operator kernels, or select specialized functional blocks. This converts a static directed acyclic graph (DAG) into an input-dependent dynamic graph.

Bengio, Emmanuel, Pierre-Luc Bacon, Joelle Pineau, and Doina Precup. 2015. “Conditional Computation in Neural Networks for Faster Models.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1511.06297.
Wang, Xin, Fisher Yu, Zi-Yi Dou, Trevor Darrell, and Joseph E. Gonzalez. 2018. “SkipNet: Learning Dynamic Routing in Convolutional Networks.” In European Conference on Computer Vision (ECCV). Springer. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-01261-8_25.

SkipNet illustrates the pattern at the granularity of whole blocks. It uses lightweight recurrent or feedforward gating controllers to conditionally bypass residual blocks in convolutional networks, preserving full network depth only for computationally difficult inputs (Wang et al. 2018). From a hardware systems perspective, conditional routing achieves wall-clock speedups only when the cycles saved by bypassing operators surpass the latency of evaluating the gating network, executing host-device control synchronization, and managing uncoalesced memory accesses.

In a dense transformer, every token computes against every weight matrix, so parameter capacity and per-token compute grow together. The mixture-of-experts (MoE) architecture decouples them by replacing dense feedforward network (FFN) layers with a bank of parallel expert subnetworks coordinated by a parameterized router (Shazeer et al. 2017). Google’s Switch Transformer14 steers each token to a single expert (\(\RoutedExpertCount=1\)), which reduces communication overhead relative to routing to \(\RoutedExpertCount> 1\) experts (Fedus et al. 2022).

14 Switch Transformer: Fedus et al. (2022) scales to roughly 1.6 trillion total parameters while routing each token to one expert; in a compute-matched experiment, Switch-Base with 64 experts reached the T5-Base quality target in about one-seventh the training time; the trillion-parameter comparison reported roughly 4\(\times\) speedup over T5-XXL. Routing each token to one expert reduces communication relative to routing to \(\RoutedExpertCount> 1\) experts, but load imbalance requires auxiliary losses and capacity factors, which cap how many tokens each expert accepts per batch. This trade-off, massive parameter capacity at low per-token compute cost in exchange for complex load-balancing engineering, defines the MoE design space.

Fedus, William, Barret Zoph, and Noam Shazeer. 2022. “Switch Transformers: Scaling to Trillion Parameter Models with Simple and Efficient Sparsity.” Journal of Machine Learning Research 23 (120): 1–39.
Lepikhin, Dmitry, HyoukJoong Lee, Yuanzhong Xu, Dehao Chen, Orhan Firat, Yanping Huang, Maxim Krikun, Noam Shazeer, and Zhifeng Chen. 2021. “GShard: Scaling Giant Models with Conditional Computation and Automatic Sharding.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

Sparse activation trades compute for memory. A token routed to one of \(\ExpertCount\) experts executes arithmetic against only \(1/\ExpertCount\) of the expert parameters, yet an all-resident deployment keeps all \(\ExpertCount\) expert matrices in device memory. In a Switch Transformer block, a Switching FFN layer sits inside the standard transformer stack, and its gating router dispatches individual tokens (“More” with \(\RouterGateProbability=0.65\), “Parameters” with \(\RouterGateProbability=0.8\)) to distinct expert sub-blocks (FFN 2 and FFN 1) while unselected experts stay idle (figure 15). Because the router lies on the critical path, the runtime must gather tokens assigned to the same expert into contiguous tiles (Lepikhin et al. 2021). At low batch sizes or under a skewed routing distribution, divergent routing leaves some experts with too few tokens to keep matrix units busy and overloads others. The runtime must then either truncate tokens or launch kernels that leave lanes idle.

↪ How runtimes execute tensors whose shapes vary, by matching precompiled shape profiles, padding, splitting the batch, or falling back to a general kernel, is developed in Dynamic kernel execution.

Yang, Le, Yizeng Han, Xi Chen, Shiji Song, Jifeng Dai, and Gao Huang. 2020. “Resolution Adaptive Networks for Efficient Inference.” 2020 IEEE/CVF Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition (CVPR), 2366–75. https://doi.org/10.1109/cvpr42600.2020.00244.
Wu, Zuxuan, Tushar Nagarajan, Abhishek Kumar, Steven Rennie, Larry S. Davis, and Rogerio Feris. 2018. “BlockDrop: Dynamic Inference Paths in Residual Networks.” IEEE/CVF Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition (CVPR), 8817–26. https://doi.org/10.1109/cvpr.2018.00919.

Early exit and MoE routing make discrete branch selections, but adaptive inference can also modulate depth or iteration counts continuously according to task difficulty (Yang et al. 2020). BlockDrop adjusts the number of active layers with a runtime policy network (Wu et al. 2018), and dynamic layer scaling adds computation until output uncertainty drops below a calibrated threshold. In an embedded vision system, high-contrast frames with clear road geometry resolve through a minimal prefix of layers, whereas degraded frames with occluded obstacles trigger deeper processing. The gain materializes only if the control evaluation remains computationally lightweight and the runtime schedules frames with matched execution depths concurrently to maintain high hardware utilization.

Implementation challenges

Realizing the efficiency gains of adaptive computation requires overcoming challenges across optimization, runtime orchestration, and hardware execution. During training, discrete routing decisions (such as non-differentiable \(\mathrm{argmax}\) branch selections) break standard gradient-based backpropagation. Practitioners overcome this with continuous relaxations such as the Gumbel-Softmax estimator, which replaces hard selections with smooth approximations so gradients flow, with policy gradients that reinforce routing choices that earn a reward, or with auxiliary load-balancing losses that keep routers from collapsing onto a single favored expert (Shazeer et al. 2017).

Shazeer, Noam, Azalia Mirhoseini, Krzysztof Maziarz, Andy Davis, Quoc Le, Geoffrey Hinton, and Jeff Dean. 2017. “Outrageously Large Neural Networks: The Sparsely-Gated Mixture-of-Experts Layer.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

At runtime, control-flow branching conflicts with the lockstep execution of modern accelerators, where the threads of a warp execute the same instruction. When threads within a GPU warp take divergent branches, the hardware may serialize them and mask inactive threads, which erodes the theoretical FLOP savings. Gating also introduces control dependencies that can limit fusion, require token-permutation buffers, or trigger host-device synchronization. If these dispatch overheads exceed the compute time saved by skipping layers, the dynamic model has higher end-to-end latency than a dense baseline. Runtime strategies such as ragged batching and dynamic kernel launch queues manage these patterns, and they belong to the accelerator runtime rather than to the model.

Dynamic control also adds quality and security failure modes. Poorly calibrated gates underallocate computation to rare inputs, which degrades accuracy on safety-critical edge cases, and adversarial perturbations can push the gates toward truncated early exits or maximally deep paths. Evaluating adaptive models therefore cannot rely on average FLOP counts alone. Rigorous system profiling must report path distribution histograms, tail latency (p99), accuracy stratified by input difficulty, and throughput stability under dynamic batching, and a production deployment keeps monitoring route frequency and quality by route after launch.

↪ Infrastructure telemetry that tracks latency distributions in deployed systems, alongside the model-quality signals production monitoring pairs with it, is developed in Infrastructure telemetry and hardware utilization.

Checkpoint 10.1: Choosing a structural method

Structural methods relocate deployment cost in different ways.

The structural techniques covered so far (pruning, low-rank factorization, distillation, efficient architecture design, NAS, and adaptive computation) all optimize what computations the model performs: which parameters exist, how layers are wired, and which paths a given input executes. Once those structural cuts have carved away redundant parameters and stages, every surviving weight and activation must still cross the memory hierarchy and execute at some numerical bit width. Optimizing that representation forms the second tier of the compression stack, and its clearest case is a workload whose time is set by bytes rather than by arithmetic.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A team prunes ResNet-50 to \(50\%\) sparsity using unstructured magnitude pruning and observes only a \(1.1\times\) speedup on a commodity GPU. Switching to structured channel pruning at the exact same \(50\%\) sparsity yields a \(1.8\times\) speedup. Which systems mechanism best explains this difference?

    1. Structured pruning removes more total weight parameters than unstructured pruning at any given nominal sparsity percentage
    2. Structured pruning removes entire contiguous channels or filters, allowing smaller dense kernels to execute efficiently, whereas unstructured sparsity needs sparse storage, metadata, and irregular activation access
    3. Unstructured magnitude pruning requires zero retraining or fine-tuning, whereas structured channel pruning requires full retraining from scratch
    4. Commodity GPU memory controllers automatically coalesce random non-zero memory addresses into single-cycle burst transfers

  2. Order the stages of finding a winning lottery ticket in a neural network according to the Lottery Ticket Hypothesis (LTH): (1) Reset surviving weights to their original initialization values (\(W_0\)), (2) Train the dense unpruned network to convergence, (3) Retrain the sparse subnetwork to convergence, (4) Prune the lowest-magnitude weights to create a sparse mask.

  3. A team must compress a large transformer model for deployment across a fleet of commodity GPUs that lack dedicated sparse-matrix acceleration kernels. Which structural optimization technique is the strongest candidate for producing a smaller model that still executes efficiently on this hardware?

    1. Unstructured magnitude pruning, because sparse matrix multiplication routines run with zero memory overhead on all standard GPUs
    2. Extreme binary weight quantization, because 1-bit representations eliminate all memory traffic without degrading language model perplexity
    3. Knowledge distillation, because it transfers teacher behavior into a compact dense student architecture that runs well on standard dense GPU kernels
    4. Low-rank factorization without fine-tuning, because mathematical decomposition guarantees zero loss in representation capacity

  4. A square weight matrix of size \(4096 \times 4096\) in a transformer layer is decomposed using low-rank factorization at rank \(r = 128\). Calculate the theoretical reduction factor in both parameter count and multiply-accumulate (MAC) operations, and explain what trade-off this structural approximation introduces.

  5. An engineering organization is deciding between running a custom Neural Architecture Search (NAS) from scratch versus adopting an established NAS-discovered family (such as MobileNetV3 or EfficientNet). Which circumstance most strongly justifies investing in custom NAS?

    1. Novel or custom hardware accelerators with unique memory hierarchies or massive production deployment scale where small per-inference efficiency gains amortize large one-time search costs
    2. Standard GPU clusters running established vision benchmarks where off-the-shelf architectures like MobileNetV3 already fit latency budgets
    3. Rapid prototyping projects with a total engineering timeline under one week and fewer than 10 available GPUs
    4. Small-scale enterprise applications processing fewer than 1,000 queries per day on cloud instances

  6. Compare one-shot pruning with an iterative schedule that removes 10 percentage points of the original weights in each of 8 rounds with recovery between rounds. Explain why staged recovery can help, and what controlled comparison is needed before concluding that the iterative schedule is better.

See Answers →

10.3 Numerical Precision: Changing How Values Are Stored

GPT-2 XL decoding on the phone shows what structural changes leave untouched. Its FP16 weights fit in the phone’s memory, yet every generated token streams all 3 GB of them across a 51.2 GB/s bus, about 59 ms per token before any arithmetic (table 1). Pruning or factorizing its layers would change which weights exist; the complementary optimization leaves the network topology unaltered and reduces the bit width of every surviving tensor. Quantization trades numerical precision for a smaller memory footprint, higher cache utilization, and higher arithmetic throughput on hardware with integer datapaths.

Definition 10.4: Quantization

Quantization is a model-compression technique that reduces information fidelity by mapping high-precision continuous values to a lower-precision discrete set.

  1. Significance: FP32-to-INT8 conversion reduces the raw payload per quantized value by 4\(\times\). Real artifact size, traffic, and latency also depend on scales, metadata, packing, operator coverage, and the target kernels.
  2. Distinction: Unlike pruning, which reduces the count of parameters, quantization reduces the bit depth of selected weights and/or activations.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that quantization is just “rounding.” In reality, it is a lossy mapping that requires careful range estimation and, often, quantization-aware training (QAT) to minimize its impact on accuracy.

↪ How accelerators trade precision and dynamic range for matrix-engine throughput, pairing low-precision inputs with higher-precision accumulation, is developed in Numerics and sparsity on the datapath.

15 Quantization: Rooted in Shannon’s theory of representing continuous signals with discrete values (Shannon 1948), reducing FP32 to INT8 collapses over four billion representable values to just 256. Neural networks often tolerate this because trained weights concentrate information in relative magnitudes, not absolute precision: INT8 inference can stay close to the full-precision baseline with appropriate calibration or quantization-aware training, while INT4 and lower-bit methods become more architecture- and method-dependent (Jacob et al. 2018; Gholami et al. 2022; Shen et al. 2020; Lin et al. 2024). The systems consequence is that quantization viability must be validated per-model and per-task, not assumed from aggregate benchmarks.

Shannon, Claude E. 1948. “A Mathematical Theory of Communication.” Bell System Technical Journal 27 (3): 379–423. https://doi.org/10.1002/j.1538-7305.1948.tb01338.x.
Jacob, Benoit, Skirmantas Kligys, Bo Chen, Menglong Zhu, Matthew Tang, Andrew Howard, Hartwig Adam, and Dmitry Kalenichenko. 2018. “Quantization and Training of Neural Networks for Efficient Integer-Arithmetic-Only Inference.” 2018 IEEE/CVF Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition, 2704–13. https://doi.org/10.1109/cvpr.2018.00286.
Gholami, Amir, Sehoon Kim, Zhen Dong, Zhewei Yao, Michael W. Mahoney, and Kurt Keutzer. 2022. “A Survey of Quantization Methods for Efficient Neural Network Inference.” In Low-Power Computer Vision: Improve the Efficiency of Artificial Intelligence. Chapman; Hall/CRC. https://doi.org/10.1201/9781003162810-13.
Gupta, Suyog, Ankur Agrawal, Kailash Gopalakrishnan, and Pritish Narayanan. 2015. “Deep Learning with Limited Numerical Precision.” International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML), 1737–46.
Wang, Yu Emma, Gu-Yeon Wei, and David Brooks. 2019. “Benchmarking TPU, GPU, and CPU Platforms for Deep Learning.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1907.10701.

Narrowing the bit width of weights and activations from FP32 (32 bits) or FP16 (16 bits) down to INT8 (8 bits) or lower15 transforms three physical terms of execution. First, converting FP32 to INT8 reduces raw weight payload by 4\(\times\) before scale and packing metadata, allowing models to fit within constrained device memories. Second, when tensors remain packed across memory buses, memory traffic drops proportionally, directly reducing token latency in bandwidth-bound autoregressive decoding. Third, fixed-point integer arithmetic units occupy smaller silicon area and consume significantly less energy per operation than floating-point units, enabling higher compute density on supported hardware (Gupta et al. 2015; Wang et al. 2019).

The accuracy cost varies by model, task, quantizer, calibration data, and bit width. Many convolutional neural network (CNN) inference models tolerate INT8 well; transformers, speech models, and outlier-heavy layers may need different granularity, mixed precision, or training-aware methods. The main approaches are post-training quantization (PTQ), quantization-aware training (QAT), and mixed-precision or extreme quantization for lower-bit deployment. They differ in training cost and control, not in a guaranteed ordering of accuracy.

The viability of each approach depends on how much precision a given model architecture can shed before quality degrades. Across precision formats from FP32 down to INT2, both CNN and transformer curves follow a low-loss plateau where INT8 quantization closely matches full-precision performance, followed by a steep accuracy cliff at lower bit widths where representational capacity breaks down. Because tolerable accuracy degradation depends on end-to-end application constraints, engineering teams must evaluate these cliff boundaries for each target workload and range-calibration recipe (figure 16).

Where that cliff sits depends first on how the target number format partitions its bits between dynamic range and precision.

10.3.1 Numerical formats

Cutting a tensor from 32 bits to 16 or 8 bits can still overflow on large activation outliers if the format lacks exponent range, or stall in software unpacking if the target processor lacks a native datapath for that bit width. How a format divides its bits between exponent range and fraction precision decides which values survive conversion, and whether the silicon provides a matching execution unit decides whether narrower values run any faster. Because neither property follows from bit count alone, selecting a numerical format requires balancing dynamic range and hardware execution support against memory traffic rather than minimizing bit width blindly. The formats commonly deployed across accelerator architectures differ in raw storage, peak arithmetic rate, and hardware support, each measured against standard 32-bit floating point (FP32) (table 7).

At 16 bits, FP16 and BF16 halve memory storage relative to FP32 while targeting dedicated matrix execution units (such as NVIDIA Tensor Cores and Google TPUs) to accelerate arithmetic throughput. The range-versus-precision trade between them is established with mixed-precision training in Mixed-precision training, and figure 17 shows the bit layouts that set it. BF16 keeps FP32’s 8-bit exponent and therefore its dynamic range, whereas FP16 spends more bits on the fraction and caps finite values at 65,504, which is why FP16 training needs loss scaling and BF16 training does not. For inference, the same range limits decide whether activations with large outliers survive the narrower format. TensorFloat-32 (TF32) adopts a hybrid compromise. It retains the 8-bit exponent of FP32 and the 10-bit fraction of FP16, executing on Tensor Cores to accelerate matrix multiplication while storing values in standard 32-bit memory.

For inference workloads, INT8 precision provides a fourfold reduction in raw storage relative to FP32 when the chosen quantizer preserves acceptable prediction accuracy. Inference avoids the gradient backpropagation pass, eliminating the small-magnitude, high-dynamic-range updates that cause underflow during training. Activations and weights can still contain outliers or sensitive values, so range calibration or QAT must validate mapping them onto signed 8-bit integers (\([-128, 127]\)) using uniform affine or symmetric quantization scales. In bandwidth-limited inference regimes, such as autoregressive language model token generation, this \(4\times\) footprint reduction relieves memory bus contention by cutting DRAM and cache traffic. On compute-bound operators, dedicated INT8 vector and tensor units provide higher arithmetic operation density per clock cycle and per unit of silicon area than equivalent floating-point units.

FP8 gets its own row because it is the first 8-bit format that keeps an exponent. It comes in two layouts, each with one sign bit: E4M3, with four exponent and three mantissa bits, for weights and activations, and E5M2, with five exponent and two mantissa bits, for the wider range of gradients (Micikevicius et al. 2022). Like INT8, it halves the bytes of FP16, and on the reference H100 its peak matrix rate equals the INT8 rate, 2× the dense FP16 peak. Unlike INT8, it needs no zero-point, and its exponent absorbs the outliers that force an integer format to choose between clipping and resolution, so a model often converts to FP8 with less range-calibration effort than to INT8. FP8 as the default serving precision on such accelerators is developed in Model Serving. Microscaling formats carry the same idea below eight bits (Rouhani et al. 2023). A block of values, typically 32, shares one 8-bit power-of-two scale, so the elements of an MXFP8, MXFP6, or MXFP4 block borrow their range from the shared exponent and spend their few bits on precision. They are an awareness item here, because their payoff depends on accelerators with native support.

Micikevicius, Paulius, Dusan Stosic, Neil Burgess, Marius Cornea, Pradeep Dubey, Richard Grisenthwaite, Sangwon Ha, et al. 2022. “FP8 Formats for Deep Learning.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2209.05433.
Rouhani, Bita Darvish, Ritchie Zhao, Ankit More, Mathew Hall, Alireza Khodamoradi, Summer Deng, Dhruv Choudhary, et al. 2023. “Microscaling Data Formats for Deep Learning.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2310.10537.

Binary and ternary formats occupy the far end of table 7, constraining values to \(\{-1, +1\}\) or \(\{-1, 0, +1\}\) so that multiplication collapses into bitwise logic or a conditional add. They promise the largest storage ratio in the table, but byte-addressed memory and training stability make them a special case that section 10.3.4 develops in full. The keyword-spotting lighthouse shows why such formats are considered at all. Under the strict ~512 KB SRAM budget, INT8 is the baseline target, and INT4, binary, or ternary representations trade additional dynamic range to meet stricter memory and power bounds. Whereas table 7 counts bytes and peak rates, what each format saves in joules depends on where its bits travel, which is the subject of the energy comparison that follows.

10.3.2 Precision and energy

In silicon, energy dissipation scales directly with wire capacitance (\(\SwitchingEnergy\propto \SwitchedCapacitance \SupplyVoltage^2\)), so shuttling bits across long memory buses costs orders of magnitude more joules than toggling logic gates inside an arithmetic logic unit. Because physical data movement so often dominates a workload’s power budget, choosing a numerical precision is an energy decision governed by the number and location of memory accesses alongside the arithmetic performed. Reducing numerical precision directly shrinks the byte volume traversing the memory hierarchy (\(\DataVolume\)) and lowers the switching energy per ALU operation (\(\EnergyPerOperation\)), relaxing memory bandwidth and thermal bottlenecks across the hardware stack.

↪ Accelerator designs that keep operands in registers and on-chip SRAM, away from costly off-chip DRAM, are developed in Machine learning hardware specialization.

Where precision saves energy

Precision saves energy in two places, and the larger one is usually movement. The energy-movement principle (7), \(\EnergyPerByteMoved \gg \EnergyPerOperation\), says that moving an operand costs far more than operating on it (Horowitz 2014). In the reference technology model, a 32-bit read from off-chip DRAM costs about 640 pJ, roughly 2,100× the energy of the INT8 multiply-accumulate that consumes it, and the cost keeps rising as data leaves the chip for storage or the network (Energy Cost of Moving vs. Computing on a 32-Bit Value). A packed INT8 stream carries one quarter of the raw value bits of an FP32 stream, so each weight or activation that crosses the memory bus costs about a quarter of the movement energy, provided transactions stay full. Caches, metadata, alignment, and transaction size set the realized saving. The reference measurements predate FP8, but an FP8 value moves the same eight bits as an INT8 value, so the movement saving applies to it unchanged.

Arithmetic supplies the second saving (table 8). An FP32 addition costs 0.9 pJ/op and an INT8 addition 0.03 pJ/op, a ratio of 30×; an FP32 multiply costs 3.7 pJ/op and an INT8 multiply 0.2 pJ/op, a ratio of 18.5×. Every multiply-accumulate a matrix unit performs combines the two, so the figure that governs a format decision is their combination. Because INT8 products accumulate in INT32, a full INT8 multiply-accumulate uses roughly 15× less energy than its FP32 equivalent, on top of the 4\(\times\) cut in bytes moved. Numbers to Know collects the reference energy ratios, and Numerical Representations compares numerical format trade-offs.

Accelerators equipped with native low-precision units (such as NVIDIA Tensor Cores, Google TPUs, or mobile NPUs) compound these arithmetic savings with reduced memory traffic. In contrast, general-purpose processors lacking native low-precision execution pipelines lose much of this benefit to the runtime overhead of bit unpacking, format conversion, and scalar fallback. Precision is therefore a model-hardware decision, not a software configuration flag.

Which saving matters depends on where the workload sits. Under the iron law of ML systems (principle 3), reducing bit width changes the data-movement \((\DataVolume/\Bandwidth)\) and compute \((\Operations/(\PeakRate \cdot \EfficiencyHw))\) terms only through the execution path that uses the new representation. Workloads with little arithmetic per byte are memory bound, so shrinking data movement dominates; workloads with more arithmetic per byte are compute bound, so arithmetic savings matter more, a distinction the roofline model in The roofline model formalizes. Neither ratio predicts battery life on its own, because end-to-end energy also includes control, sensors, and idle power. When memory capacity binds, the more immediate benefit is placement, because smaller values may let the model reside in a memory tier that the uncompressed model cannot fit.

The machine’s memory capacity, not arithmetic, now sets the constraint.

Recommendation models convert the energy argument into a placement problem, in which quantization must shrink the dominant memory structures enough to reside in the host machine’s physical memory. When an embedding table outgrows a single accelerator’s HBM, reducing bytes per entry determines whether the table fits on the node at all.

Lighthouse 10.1: DLRM and embedding quantization
The DLRM lighthouse presents a compression challenge where memory capacity, rather than compute throughput or memory bandwidth, is the binding constraint (Naumov et al. 2019). Even the reference configuration in table 1 needs 100 GB of weights, more than one accelerator’s memory, and production embedding tables can grow to terabytes that must be sharded across many devices.

For DLRM, quantization targets storage density rather than arithmetic throughput. Reducing embedding-table precision from FP32 to INT8 (or lower) shrinks the memory footprint by 4–8\(\times\), allowing larger tables to fit on fewer GPUs when accuracy and lookup kernels tolerate the lower precision. Compressing the lookup tables ensures that physical device memory can accommodate the algorithmic model.

Naumov, Maxim, Dheevatsa Mudigere, Hao-Jun Michael Shi, Jianyu Huang, Narayanan Sundaraman, Jongsoo Park, Xiaodong Wang, et al. 2019. “Deep Learning Recommendation Model for Personalization and Recommendation Systems.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1906.00091.

Lighthouse 10.2: Keyword spotting and the SRAM budget
The KWS lighthouse, DS-CNN, operates at the opposite extreme of the iron law from DLRM (Y. Zhang et al. 2017). Consider a deployment that keeps weights in roughly 512 KB of SRAM. Even the purpose-built DS-CNN keyword spotter overshoots that budget in FP32, where its weights occupy 800 KB. Quantized to INT8, its weights shrink to 200 KB, leaving space whose sufficiency must be checked against peak activation and runtime-buffer use. A device that reads weights directly from flash has a separate weight-storage budget; its SRAM feasibility still depends on the working set. A stricter smart-doorbell microcontroller with 256 KB SRAM leaves closer to 100 KB for model weights after runtime buffers, audio windows, and feature extraction state, motivating more aggressive INT4 or binary compression.

In FP32, even the compact DS-CNN architecture moves 4\(\times\) more raw weight bytes than in INT8, and the arithmetic path can also cost more on hardware with efficient integer units. For an always-on coin-cell device, that reduction contributes to longer battery life, but sensor, wake-up, feature-extraction, and idle-power costs may dominate. Here, quantization reduces the model’s data-movement term and can improve its compute term \((\Operations / (\PeakRate \cdot \EfficiencyHw))\) when the integer path is supported.

Zhang, Yundong, Naveen Suda, Liangzhen Lai, and Vikas Chandra. 2017. Hello Edge: Keyword Spotting on Microcontrollers.

Together, DLRM and the TinyML lighthouse (10.2) illustrate the two operational extremes of this memory constraint. DLRM uses lower precision to fit embedding tables that can reach terabytes into host memory; TinyML uses lower precision to keep every inference within a tiny energy envelope and a few hundred kilobytes of on-chip SRAM. Across both extremes, reducing numerical precision attacks physical execution costs on two fronts, arithmetic energy and latency in the ALU and byte traffic across the memory bus. Both savings assume that the low-precision values still represent the model faithfully, and producing such values is the job of the quantization methods.

10.3.3 Quantization methods

Casting trained FP32 weights and activations straight to narrow integers without fitting their range clips outlier channels and rounds small values to zero, so practical quantization methods differ in how much calibration or retraining they spend to keep that error small. Post-training quantization, quantization-aware training, and extreme low-bit formats differ chiefly in the training and validation cost they require. Representation choices, such as selecting INT8 versus INT4, mixing bit widths across sensitive layers, or choosing per-tensor versus per-channel ranges, are orthogonal options evaluated within these workflows rather than separate maturity levels. Ordered by the engineering effort each requires, the deployment sequence moves from fast post-training range calibration through quantization-aware retraining to custom low-bit pipelines (figure 18).

Post-training quantization is evaluated first, because it changes representations with minimal compute and pipeline overhead. When PTQ satisfies the application’s latency, energy, and quality thresholds, the optimization process stops. When post-training calibration introduces unacceptable accuracy degradation, quantization-aware training lets the weights adapt to discretization noise during backpropagation, and extreme sub-4-bit or ternary representations are reserved for targets that INT8 and INT4 cannot meet and whose hardware has kernels for them.

Post-training quantization

Retraining a large model checkpoint solely to adapt its weights to 8-bit arithmetic can cost hundreds of accelerator hours, and in many deployment pipelines the original training dataset or training cluster is not even available. Post-training quantization (PTQ) avoids that retraining loop altogether by converting weights and activation tensors from floating-point to fixed-point formats directly on a frozen checkpoint (Choukroun et al. 2019). Weight-only quantization requires only a single pass over static parameter tensors, while full integer quantization calibrates activation dynamic ranges using a small, unlabelled sample of representative inputs.

The principal constraint in PTQ is unmodeled discretization noise. Rounding introduces numerical error that cannot be compensated by gradient updates. While redundant architectures absorb 8-bit rounding noise with negligible accuracy loss, sensitive models or lower bit widths (such as INT4) experience degradation unless scaling granularities are refined or quantization-aware training is applied. Production runtimes such as TensorFlow Lite, Open Neural Network Exchange (ONNX) Runtime, and TensorRT provide automated calibration pipelines to profile tensor ranges and apply this conversion.

The core mechanism of PTQ is uniform quantization, which maps floating-point values to discrete integer levels using a consistent scaling factor. Because the interval between each quantized value is constant, uniform quantization simplifies implementation and enables efficient hardware execution. For the symmetric form shown here, \(\QuantizationScale\) is chosen from the maximum absolute value so that the resulting integers stay within the target range. The quantized value \(\QuantizedIntegerCode\) is computed as: \[ \QuantizedIntegerCode = \text{round} \left(\frac{\QuantizerInput}{\QuantizationScale} \right) \] where:

  • \(\QuantizedIntegerCode\) is the quantized integer representation,
  • \(\QuantizerInput\) is the original floating-point value,
  • \(\QuantizationScale\) is a scaling factor that maps the floating-point range to the available integer range.

Uniform quantization from FP32 to INT8 reduces the raw value payload from 32 to 8 bits per weight at the cost of a measurable quantization error, with scale metadata and packing overhead excluded (listing 2). Once a model is quantized, the runtime can use integer arithmetic where supported (Gholami et al. 2022).

Rounding to a uniform grid introduces an error with a precise bound. A uniform quantizer maps each value to the nearest of evenly spaced levels separated by a step size \(\QuantizationScale\) (range calibration derives \(\QuantizationScale\) from the tensor’s clipping range in equation 1, later in this section). For any value inside the clipping range, rounding to the nearest level misses by at most half a step, so within-range error is bounded by \([-\QuantizationScale/2, \QuantizationScale/2]\). Aggregated across many tensors, most error concentrates inside that bound, while the tails carry the larger distortion that comes from saturation clipping and from mixing tensors that use different step sizes (figure 19). Many CNNs tolerate INT8 well, whereas large transformers often need outlier-aware or mixed-precision schemes because a few activation channels carry extreme values. Selecting the appropriate numerical precision therefore requires balancing accuracy constraints, hardware support, and efficiency gains.

An alternative, nonuniform quantization, assigns finer-grained precision to numerical ranges that are more densely populated, which can preserve accuracy for models whose weight distributions concentrate around specific values. Nonuniform schemes require more complex calibration and are less common in production, but they can be effective for models particularly sensitive to precision changes. More generally, sensitivity to PTQ varies by architecture, layer, and distribution. When a uniform configuration misses the quality target, the next options include finer granularity, mixed precision, weight-only methods, better calibration, or QAT.

Range calibration

Mapping a tensor onto 8-bit or 4-bit integers with an ill-fitting range either saturates critical values through clipping or squanders available integer levels on sparse outliers. Quantization-range calibration prevents both failures by estimating the real-valued clipping range \([\ClippingRangeLowerBound, \ClippingRangeUpperBound]\) for each tensor (from here on, \(\ClippingRangeLowerBound\) and \(\ClippingRangeUpperBound\) denote its lower and upper bounds). Calibration minimizes representation error over the sampled distribution, although it does not guarantee bounds on out-of-distribution deployment inputs.

Because activation tensors vary with input data, profiling requires running an unlabelled calibration dataset through the forward pass to capture dynamic ranges. In the offline conversion pipeline, the pretrained model and a representative calibration dataset pass through a profiling stage that establishes dynamic ranges, and those ranges parameterize the quantization step that yields the final quantized model (figure 20). Layer by layer, activation observers record value distributions from which the scale and zero-point metadata are computed (algorithm 1). Range calibration in this sense is distinct from the confidence calibration that asks whether a model’s predicted probabilities match its observed accuracy, which returns in section 10.6.

\(f_\theta\)

The single calibration pass over \(\CalibrationDataset\) avoids retraining, and FP32-to-INT8 weight quantization cuts raw stored weight values by 4\(\times\) before scale, zero-point, and packing metadata. The price is that the static activation ranges fixed in the loop risk saturation or wasted integer levels when the calibration data misses deployment tails. The quantization step then converts model parameters to the lower-precision format, producing the final quantized model.

Mapping an unnecessarily wide real-valued interval to the 256 discrete bins of an 8-bit integer wastes numerical resolution on empty range, whereas clipping too aggressively truncates signal. Three calibration methods balance this clipping-versus-resolution trade-off. The max method sets bounds to the absolute extremum, which preserves extreme values at the cost of vulnerability to outliers. The entropy method selects bounds by minimizing the Kullback–Leibler (KL) divergence between original and quantized distributions. The percentile method retains a chosen fraction (such as 99.9 percent) and clips the remaining tail. In one ResNet-50 layer, the dense bulk of activations clusters below 1.1, while a sparse outlier tail stretching past 2.0 motivates testing a candidate clipping boundary near 1.1 to allocate more INT8 bins to the bulk (figure 21). Choosing a percentile boundary requires cumulative activation counts; the illustrated boundary is not assigned a percentile rank. Task validation must establish whether clipping that tail preserves important rare cases.

Tuning quantization ranges

Once calibration identifies clipping boundaries \([\ClippingRangeLowerBound, \ClippingRangeUpperBound]\), the range must be mapped onto integer endpoints, and two mapping schemes dominate (figure 22). A symmetric mapping uses a zero-centered range with zero-point zero, forcing real zero to integer zero by mapping \([-1, 1]\) directly onto \([-127, 127]\). An asymmetric mapping keeps one scale but a generally nonzero zero-point \(\QuantizationZeroPoint\), which accommodates skewed activation intervals such as \([-0.5, 1.5]\) across \([-128, 127]\).

Symmetric quantization simplifies integer matrix multiplication kernels because the zero-point is zero (\(\QuantizationZeroPoint= 0\)), eliminating cross-term additions during hardware accumulation. However, for activations following asymmetric distributions (such as post-ReLU feature maps where all values are non-negative), symmetric quantization wastes roughly half of the integer dynamic range on unused negative values. Asymmetric quantization recovers this dynamic range by mapping \([\ClippingRangeLowerBound, \ClippingRangeUpperBound]\) to \([0, 2^\QuantizationBitWidth- 1]\), at the cost of extra arithmetic inside the dot product. Because each real activation is proportional to its quantized integer minus the activation zero-point \(\QuantizationZeroPoint\), multiplying shifted activations by quantized weights \(\QuantizedWeightEntry\) expands each output row \(\MatrixRowIndex\) into the standard integer dot product minus a cross-term \(\QuantizationZeroPoint\sum_k \QuantizedWeightEntry\). When both the weights and the activation zero-point are fixed offline, a compiler can precompute \(\QuantizationZeroPoint\sum_k \QuantizedWeightEntry\) for each output channel and fold that constant offset into the layer’s 32-bit bias. When the zero-point varies at runtime or when weights themselves use asymmetric zero-points, hardware must evaluate the extra reduction pass during inference. Either mapping is fully specified by two numbers per range, a scale and a zero-point, and the uniform quantizer introduced for PTQ is the special case whose zero-point is zero.

Napkin Math 10.1: Calculating scale and zero-point
The affine quantization formula: An affine map sends a floating-point range \([\ClippingRangeLowerBound, \ClippingRangeUpperBound]\) that includes zero to an integer range \([0, 2^\QuantizationBitWidth-1]\) (for example, UINT8) via the following construction.

Analysis: Affine quantization establishes a linear mapping \(\QuantizerInput\approx \QuantizationScale(\QuantizedIntegerCode - \QuantizationZeroPoint)\), where \(\QuantizationScale\) is the scale (step size) and \(\QuantizationZeroPoint\) is the zero-point (integer value corresponding to real zero). The mapping involves three computational steps:

  1. Calculate scale \((\QuantizationScale)\): Divide the real range by the integer range using equation 1. \[\QuantizationScale= \frac{\ClippingRangeUpperBound- \ClippingRangeLowerBound}{2^\QuantizationBitWidth- 1} \tag{1}\]

  2. Calculate zero-point \((\QuantizationZeroPoint)\): Shift the range so that real zero maps to an integer using equation 2. \[\QuantizationZeroPoint= \text{round}\left(\frac{-\ClippingRangeLowerBound}{\QuantizationScale}\right) \tag{2}\]

  3. Quantize \((\QuantizerInput\to \QuantizedIntegerCode)\): Divide by the scale, add the zero-point, then round and clamp to the integer range using equation 3. \[\QuantizedIntegerCode = \text{clamp}\left(\text{round}\left(\frac{\QuantizerInput}{\QuantizationScale} + \QuantizationZeroPoint\right), 0, 2^\QuantizationBitWidth- 1\right) \tag{3}\]

Scenario: Consider quantizing a \(2 \times 2\) activation matrix with values ranging from \(\ClippingRangeLowerBound = -1\) to \(\ClippingRangeUpperBound = 3\), and the target precision is UINT8 \((\QuantizationBitWidth=8)\):

\[\mathbf{X} = \begin{pmatrix} -1 & 1 \\ 0 & 3 \end{pmatrix}\]

  • Range: \(\ClippingRangeUpperBound - \ClippingRangeLowerBound = 4\).
  • Steps: \(2^8 - 1\) = 255.
  • Scale: \(\QuantizationScale = \frac{4}{255} \approx 0.0157\).
  • Zero-point: \(\QuantizationZeroPoint = \text{round}(-(\ClippingRangeLowerBound)/\QuantizationScale) = \text{round}\!\left(-(-1)/\frac{4}{255}\right) = \text{round}(63.75) = 64\).

Applying equation 3 element-wise maps each floating-point entry to an integer bin:

\[\mathbf{X}_q = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & 128 \\ 64 & 255 \end{pmatrix}\]

Systems insight: The real value \(0.0\) is represented exactly by the integer 64. This is critical because it ensures that zero-padding (common in CNNs) is represented exactly, preventing “quantization drift” where padding introduces nonzero noise.

Granularity

In convolutional filters or transformer projection matrices, weight magnitudes often vary widely from one channel to the next, so sharing a single scale factor across an entire tensor can collapse small-magnitude channels to zero or clip wider channels. Quantization granularity governs how broadly scale factors and zero-points are shared across a tensor. It balances representation resolution against metadata overhead and kernel complexity. Across convolutional channels (filters 1 through \(\LayerOutputWidth\)), a global layerwise threshold spans the widest distribution at the cost of resolution in narrower filters. Per-channel clipping boundaries, by contrast, adapt tightly to each filter’s dynamic range at the cost of tracking additional scale vectors during matrix multiplication (figure 23).

Quantization granularity trades calibration and metadata overhead against representation error. More local ranges can preserve information when channels differ, but they do not improve accuracy automatically and may lack an efficient kernel path. Each additional scale also has to be stored, loaded, and applied at the granularity supported by the backend. Four levels are common, from one shared layer range to local ranges within a filter (table 9).

Channelwise quantization preserves accuracy over layerwise quantization when channel dynamic ranges differ, at the cost of storing one scale per output filter and applying per-channel rescaling in the kernel epilogue. Because each output channel is an independent dot product accumulated into its own 32-bit register, multiplying by a per-channel weight scale happens only once per output element after the inner reduction loop finishes. Sub-channelwise (per-group) quantization, common in 4-bit weight compression for large language models, slices the inner reduction dimension itself into blocks of 32 to 128 weights that each carry their own scale. The kernel must therefore rescale partial sums inside the inner loop rather than waiting for the final accumulator. Beyond scale granularity, execution efficiency depends on the distinction between static weights and dynamic activations, which impose different memory and latency constraints on the deployment system.

Weights vs. activations
