Executing low-precision inference requires transforming floating-point inputs before kernel execution and requantizing intermediate accumulator outputs. During integer matrix multiplication, multiplying two 8-bit integers produces a 16-bit product. Summing these products across an inner dimension of length \(\GemmInnerDimension\) causes bit-growth that quickly overflows 8-bit registers; hardware therefore accumulates into 32-bit integer registers (INT32). Before feeding the result into subsequent layers, requantization rescales the 32-bit accumulated sum by the layer’s effective scale factor (\(\RequantizationScale= \frac{\WeightQuantizationScale \cdot \InputQuantizationScale}{\OutputQuantizationScale}\)) and clamps the values back into the 8-bit integer range. The full pipeline therefore has four stages (figure 24): quantizers convert floating-point activations and weights into INT8 operands, an integer core executes the matrix multiplication, a 32-bit accumulator holds the sum, and a requantization stage returns the result to INT8 before the nonlinear activation.

↪ How a single Tensor Core matrix instruction takes low-precision input tiles into higher-precision accumulator registers is developed in Tensor Cores.

Activation quantization maps layer outputs to a lower-precision representation during inference. It reduces intermediate payloads and can reduce arithmetic cost on hardware with an efficient integer path, but it also introduces error between layers. For example, a CNN may convert FP32 feature maps to INT8 before later convolutions. The benefit is greatest when consecutive operators remain on the integer path; isolated quantized operators may pay conversion cost without reducing enough work. Whether activation quantization improves latency therefore depends on supported kernels, scale handling, tensor lifetimes, and any quantize or dequantize boundaries that remain in the exported graph.

Static vs. dynamic quantization

The clipping range also depends on when activation ranges are computed, which divides activation quantization into two primary execution models: static quantization and dynamic quantization. In static quantization, the clipping range is precalculated during offline calibration and remains fixed during inference. This approach introduces zero runtime range-estimation overhead, because inputs are scaled by fixed factors and directly fed to integer execution units. However, static ranges risk saturation or underflow whenever production inputs deviate from the calibration distribution (Jacob et al. 2018; Yao et al. 2021).

Yao, Zhewei, Amir Gholami, Sheng Shen, Kurt Keutzer, and Michael W. Mahoney. 2021. “HAWQ-V3: Dyadic Neural Network Quantization.” Proceedings of the 38th International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML), 11875–86.

Dynamic quantization instead calculates scaling factors and zero-points dynamically from runtime activation tensors. Adapting the clipping window to each input reduces mismatch with a fixed calibration range. Outliers can still consume resolution, and a policy that clips tails can still saturate values. The trade-off is runtime memory and latency overhead, because the hardware must perform reduction passes over intermediate activation tensors to extract minimum and maximum values, quantize the floating-point activations into integers on the fly, and only then launch the integer matrix multiplication. On memory-bandwidth-bound operators, this reduction pass can erode the execution advantage of lower precision. Dynamic integer quantization is also distinct from weight-only quantization (such as 4-bit weights with 16-bit floating-point activations), which skips activation quantization altogether and instead dequantizes compressed weights into floating-point registers inside the matrix-multiplication kernel.

Checkpoint 10.2: Calibration and range choices

Quantization succeeds only when the range policy matches deployment data and runtime constraints.

Range design

PTQ in practice

Post-training quantization is the default starting point in production because it needs only a small calibration set and minutes of compute rather than a retraining pipeline. Whether offline calibration suffices depends on the architecture’s outlier structure, the calibration data’s coverage, the scale granularity, and the target bit width.

Its fundamental ceiling is that PTQ freezes the trained weights: calibration can move clipping bounds and refine per-channel or per-group scales, but it cannot move parameters to compensate for rounding error. When recalibration, finer scale granularity, and mixed-precision overrides still leave a candidate below its quality floor, the remaining lever is to let the weights adapt during training at the cost of additional training compute (\(\TrainingCompute\)) and hyperparameter tuning.

Quantization-aware training

When post-training quantization drops a sensitive architecture or an aggressive 4-bit target below its accuracy floor, only gradient updates can recover the lost quality. Quantization-aware training (QAT) incorporates numerical rounding noise directly into the optimization graph (section 10.3.3.2.1), retraining or fine-tuning a checkpoint on the training dataset so its parameters adapt to low-precision discretization error (figure 25).

Rather than training from scratch or cold-starting scale parameters, production workflows frequently initialize QAT using scale factors derived during an initial PTQ calibration pass. In this two-phase pipeline, a PTQ phase feeds calibration data into the pretrained model to establish initial scale metadata, and the resulting PTQ checkpoint enters a QAT phase in which the full training data drives fine-tuning (figure 26).

Training mathematics

During forward propagation, weights and activations are quantized and dequantized to mimic reduced precision. Let \(\QuantizerInput\) be a full-precision value, \(\QuantizationScale\) the scaling factor that maps floating-point values into a lower-precision range, and \(\FakeQuantizedOutput\) the simulated (quantized, then dequantized) value. Ignoring clipping and the zero-point for this simplified expression, the process is represented as: \[ \FakeQuantizedOutput= \text{round} \left(\frac{\QuantizerInput}{\QuantizationScale} \right) \times \QuantizationScale\]

Although the forward pass executes with simulated quantization, computing gradients requires backpropagating through the rounding operator \(\text{round}(\cdot)\). Because rounding is a piecewise-constant step function, its mathematical derivative is zero almost everywhere and undefined at step transitions, which would zero out gradients and halt gradient descent. The straight-through estimator (STE) resolves this bottleneck by substituting the identity function for the rounding operator during backpropagation (Bengio et al. 2013).16

16 Straight-through estimator (STE): Proposed by Bengio et al. (2013), the common identity STE copies the upstream gradient through the quantizer unchanged. Because that identity is not the derivative of rounding, the estimate is biased, so QAT descends an approximation of the quantized model’s loss surface rather than the surface itself.

Bengio, Yoshua, Nicholas Léonard, and Aaron Courville. 2013. “Estimating or Propagating Gradients Through Stochastic Neurons for Conditional Computation.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1308.3432.
Krishnamoorthi, Raghuraman. 2018. “Quantizing Deep Convolutional Networks for Efficient Inference: A Whitepaper.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1806.08342 abs/1806.08342.

Integrating quantization effects during training lets the model adapt its weights and activation ranges to simulated low-precision error. QAT can recover quality that a post-training route loses, particularly at aggressive bit widths, but the advantage depends on the model, quantizer, calibration, and training procedure (Krishnamoorthi 2018).

Fake quantization nodes and implementation

QAT implementation relies on fake quantization operations that simulate quantization during forward propagation while maintaining full precision for gradient computation. These operations insert quantize-dequantize pairs into the computational graph, creating a training-time simulation of inference-time behavior.

A fake quantization node must preserve the same errors the inference runtime will see, so its three operations model the deployment path during training:

  1. Quantization: Map floating-point value to discrete quantization level
  2. Clipping: Enforce range constraints based on bit width
  3. Dequantization: Convert back to floating-point for subsequent operations

Mathematically, for symmetric quantization with bit width \(\QuantizationBitWidth\), given a floating-point input value \(\QuantizerInput\): \[ \begin{aligned} \QuantizedIntegerCode &= \text{clip}\left(\text{round}\left(\frac{\QuantizerInput}{\QuantizationScale}\right), -2^{\QuantizationBitWidth-1}, 2^{\QuantizationBitWidth-1} - 1\right) \\ \FakeQuantizedOutput&= \QuantizedIntegerCode \times \QuantizationScale\end{aligned} \] where \(\QuantizedIntegerCode\) is the quantized integer level, \(\QuantizationScale= \frac{\max(|\QuantizerInput|)}{2^{\QuantizationBitWidth-1} - 1}\) is the scale factor computed from the input distribution, and \(\FakeQuantizedOutput\) represents the fake-quantized output that mimics INT8 values but remains in floating-point format.

For asymmetric quantization supporting unsigned integers, the node applies the scale, zero-point, and quantize steps of equation 1, equation 2, and equation 3 to the observed range (extended to include zero) and then dequantizes: \[ \begin{aligned} \QuantizationScale&= \frac{\max(\QuantizerInput) - \min(\QuantizerInput)}{2^\QuantizationBitWidth- 1} \\ \QuantizationZeroPoint&= \text{round}\left(-\frac{\min(\QuantizerInput)}{\QuantizationScale}\right) \\ \QuantizedIntegerCode &= \text{clip}\left(\text{round}\left(\frac{\QuantizerInput}{\QuantizationScale} + \QuantizationZeroPoint\right), 0, 2^\QuantizationBitWidth- 1\right) \\ \FakeQuantizedOutput&= (\QuantizedIntegerCode - \QuantizationZeroPoint) \times \QuantizationScale\end{aligned} \] where \(\QuantizationZeroPoint\) is the zero-point offset enabling asymmetric range representation and the observed range \([\min(\QuantizerInput), \max(\QuantizerInput)]\) serves as the clipping range \([\ClippingRangeLowerBound, \ClippingRangeUpperBound]\).

In the QAT forward path, fake quantization is applied to inputs and weights before convolution, simulating INT8 numerics while retaining floating-point tensors for training (listing 3).

During backpropagation, fake quantization nodes approximate gradients through a clipping-aware straight-through estimator: \[ \frac{\partial \FakeQuantizedOutput}{\partial \QuantizerInput} = \begin{cases} 1 & \text{if } \QuantizerInput\in [\ClippingRangeLowerBound, \ClippingRangeUpperBound] \\ 0 & \text{otherwise} \end{cases} \] Within the clipping range \([\ClippingRangeLowerBound, \ClippingRangeUpperBound]\), the estimator treats quantization as an identity mapping, passing the upstream gradient \(\frac{\partial \LossFunction}{\partial \FakeQuantizedOutput}\) unchanged to master weight \(\QuantizerInput\). Beyond clipping boundaries, the derivative evaluates to zero, blocking gradients from saturated outliers that would otherwise destabilize optimizer updates.

In practice, frameworks like PyTorch and TensorFlow implement fake quantization as custom autograd operators whose forward pass performs the quantize-dequantize round trip while the backward pass applies an STE. Scale handling depends on the method, since observers may track distributions and later freeze, or scales may be learned. Batch normalization also requires deployment-consistent handling. Workflows freeze batch-normalization statistics and fold them into the preceding convolution before export (section 10.4.1). Freezing running statistics before export allows fake-quantization scales to match the static fused parameters executed during inference.

QAT trade-offs

QAT’s17 primary advantage is additional control over quality at low precision. By incorporating simulated quantization noise during training or fine-tuning, the model can adapt to the deployed numerical path. Processors with dedicated integer units can then exploit INT8 arithmetic for faster or lower-energy inference when the exported operators are supported (Wu et al. 2020; Gholami et al. 2022). The benefit must be measured against PTQ for the same model and target, including the extra training cost and any difference in calibration, graph coverage, or kernel selection.

17 Quantization-aware training (QAT): QAT simulates low-precision behavior during training or fine-tuning so weight updates can adapt to clipping and rounding error. BERT studies report that training-aware or Hessian-aware methods can remain closer to full-precision General Language Understanding Evaluation (GLUE) baselines than simpler post-training routes in their evaluated settings (Zafrir et al. 2019; Shen et al. 2020). The exact gap depends on model, task, quantizer, bit width, training budget, and evaluation distribution; a closer benchmark result is evidence for trying QAT, not a guarantee that its added training cost or exported runtime will meet a production target.

Zafrir, Ofir, Guy Boudoukh, Peter Izsak, and Moshe Wasserblat. 2019. “Q8BERT: Quantized 8Bit BERT.” 2019 Fifth Workshop on Energy Efficient Machine Learning and Cognitive Computing - NeurIPS Edition (EMC2-NIPS), 36–39. https://doi.org/10.1109/emc2-nips53020.2019.00016.
Shen, Sheng, Zhen Dong, Jiayu Ye, Linjian Ma, Zhewei Yao, Amir Gholami, Michael W. Mahoney, and Kurt Keutzer. 2020. “Q-BERT: Hessian Based Ultra Low Precision Quantization of BERT.” Proceedings of the AAAI Conference on Artificial Intelligence 34 (05): 8815–21. https://doi.org/10.1609/aaai.v34i05.6409.
Wu, Hao, Patrick Judd, Xiaojie Zhang, Mikhail Isaev, and Paulius Micikevicius. 2020. “Integer Quantization for Deep Learning Inference: Principles and Empirical Evaluation.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2004.09602 abs/2004.09602.
Choukroun, Yoni, Eli Kravchik, Fan Yang, and Pavel Kisilev. 2019. “Low-Bit Quantization of Neural Networks for Efficient Inference.” 2019 IEEE/CVF International Conference on Computer Vision Workshop (ICCVW), 3009–18. https://doi.org/10.1109/iccvw.2019.00363.

The cost is additional engineering complexity. QAT inserts simulated quantization into training, so teams must validate quantization schemes, scale behavior, and accuracy recovery on the target model. For very large models whose training budgets are already constrained, that overhead can make QAT impractical, and post-training routes avoid retraining altogether (Choukroun et al. 2019). QAT is therefore warranted only when the exported PTQ model misses its quality or deployment targets and training resources permit. A hybrid approach, starting with PTQ calibration and applying QAT fine-tuning only to accuracy-critical layers, often provides a useful balance.

The choice among PTQ, QAT, and dynamic quantization comes down to engineering budget, accuracy threshold, and input variability (table 10). Dynamic range computation is a timing choice orthogonal to PTQ versus QAT (dynamic PTQ is common), but it belongs in the comparison because it answers the third of those questions.

PTQ serves as the low-effort baseline; QAT spends training cycles when PTQ degrades task accuracy, while dynamic quantization expends runtime memory traffic and compute to track input-dependent dynamic ranges. In practice, PTQ and QAT commonly target 8-bit or 4-bit precision, where retained accuracy depends on the model, task, and quantizer. Some deployment scenarios, however, demand even more aggressive compression, pushing precision to the absolute limits of what neural networks can tolerate.

10.3.4 Extreme quantization

On a microcontroller whose runtime buffers and audio feature extraction leave barely 100 KB of SRAM for weights, or on an energy-harvesting sensor whose power envelope cannot afford hardware multipliers, even 8-bit and 4-bit integers exceed the deployment budget. Extreme quantization pushes numerical precision down to 1 or 2 bits per value for those targets (Courbariaux et al. 2015; Zhu et al. 2017). Binarization constrains weights and activations to two values, typically encoded as \(\{-1, +1\}\). In digital logic, mapping \(-1\) to binary \(0\) and \(+1\) to binary \(1\) renders scalar multiplication isomorphic to the bitwise XNOR operation, replacing floating-point or integer MAC units with bitwise logic and population count (popcount) instructions (Rastegari et al. 2016). Because a 64-bit register packs 64 binary values, a single 64-bit XNOR instruction computes 64 binary products in parallel. A hardware popcount instruction then counts the matching bits to accumulate the dot product (\(2 \times \text{popcount}(\BinaryWeightVector\text{ XNOR } \BinaryActivationVector) - 64\)), which eliminates hardware multipliers altogether. That efficiency comes at a steep cost in expressiveness, since 1-bit representations degrade accuracy sharply on complex perceptual and language tasks (Hubara et al. 2018).

Courbariaux, Matthieu, Yoshua Bengio, and Jean-Pierre David. 2015. “BinaryConnect: Training Deep Neural Networks with Binary Weights During Propagations.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 28: 3123–31.

Ternarization mitigates this capacity loss by allowing three values: \(\{-1, 0, +1\}\) (Zhu et al. 2017). The zero state introduces sparsity and simplifies arithmetic. Multiplying an activation by \(+1\) requires an addition, multiplying by \(-1\) requires a subtraction, and multiplying by \(0\) is skipped entirely. This eliminates general-purpose multipliers, reducing the arithmetic unit to a conditional adder-subtractor accumulator. However, ternary values cannot be packed at one bit per value. They require at least two bits per weight in standard byte-aligned memory (wasting one of four possible 2-bit states) or multi-trit encoding (such as packing five trits into a single byte, since \(3^5 = 243 < 256\)) that introduces runtime decoding overhead. The arithmetic gains of multiplier-free execution must outweigh the instruction overhead required to decode and unpack the weights on the target architecture.

Both binary and ternary quantization introduce step functions whose derivative is zero almost everywhere (\(\frac{\DerivativeOperator}{d\QuantizerInput}\text{sign}(\QuantizerInput) = 0\) for \(\QuantizerInput\neq 0\)), causing backpropagation gradients to vanish. To train these representations, quantization-aware training again relies on the straight-through estimator (Bengio et al. 2013; Choi et al. 2018) of section 10.3.3.2.1, whose gradients reach full-precision latent weights that accumulate small optimizer steps until they cross the quantization thresholds.

Choi, Jungwook, Zhuo Wang, Swagath Venkataramani, Pierce I-Jen Chuang, Vijayalakshmi Srinivasan, and Kailash Gopalakrishnan. 2018. “PACT: Parameterized Clipping Activation for Quantized Neural Networks.” arXiv Preprint.

Challenges and limitations

Translating sub-byte precision into actual wall-clock speedups is constrained by standard processor architectures. General-purpose memory hierarchies are byte-addressed and vector ALUs operate on 8-bit, 16-bit, or 32-bit lanes, so native support for binary or two-bit matrix operations is rare outside specialized engines. Moreover, when 1-bit or 2-bit weights multiply 8-bit or 16-bit activations, or when four 2-bit ternary weights are packed into a single byte, bitwise XNOR logic no longer applies. On a target without a matching sub-byte datapath, the kernel must load each packed byte into a register and execute bit-shifting, masking, and sign-extension instructions to unpack every 1-bit or 2-bit weight into an 8-bit or 16-bit lane before the vector multiplier can run. The instruction overhead of this bit manipulation frequently offsets memory-bandwidth savings unless workloads run on custom bit-serial accelerators, field-programmable gate arrays (FPGAs), or specialized hardware with native bitwise execution units (Umuroglu et al. 2017).

Umuroglu, Yaman, Nicholas J. Fraser, Giulio Gambardella, Michaela Blott, Philip Leong, Magnus Jahre, and Kees Vissers. 2017. “Finn: A Framework for Fast, Scalable Binarized Neural Network Inference.” Proceedings of the 2017 ACM/SIGDA International Symposium on Field-Programmable Gate Arrays, 65–74. https://doi.org/10.1145/3020078.3021744.
Rastegari, Mohammad, Vicente Ordonez, Joseph Redmon, and Ali Farhadi. 2016. “XNOR-Net: ImageNet Classification Using Binary Convolutional Neural Networks.” In European Conference on Computer Vision (ECCV). Springer. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-46493-0_32.
Hubara, Itay, Matthieu Courbariaux, Daniel Soudry, Ran El-Yaniv, and Yoshua Bengio. 2018. “Quantized Neural Networks: Training Neural Networks with Low Precision Weights and Activations.” Journal of Machine Learning Research 18 (187): 1–30.
Zhu, Chenzhuo, Song Han, Huizi Mao, and William J. Dally. 2017. “Trained Ternary Quantization.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR) 4 (4): 1–16.

Model quality is the second barrier. Extreme quantization sharply reduces the representational capacity of a network. While low-entropy workloads such as KWS on a microcontroller SRAM budget may tolerate binary or ternary weights, complex perceptual and generative models suffer steep accuracy degradation. Binary and ternary quantization become viable candidates only when higher-precision alternatives such as INT8 or INT4 fail to satisfy physical memory or energy budgets, and when the deployment hardware can execute bit-packed operations natively (Rastegari et al. 2016; Hubara et al. 2018; Zhu et al. 2017; Umuroglu et al. 2017). Production deployment requires evaluating binary and ternary baselines against INT8, INT4, structured pruning, and compact architectural redesigns on the identical workload, measuring total binary artifact size, end-to-end latency, energy per inference, and task accuracy on target hardware. Whether a narrower format actually runs faster is the last question the precision tier must answer.

10.3.5 When fewer bits run faster

Shrinking weights from 16 bits to 8 bits fails to speed up a compute-bound matrix multiplication if the processor lacks a faster 8-bit datapath, just as faster integer ALUs fail to speed up autoregressive decoding when latency is stalled on memory-bus transfers. Every byte that quantization removes is a byte the memory system no longer moves, so whether a quantized model runs faster depends on which hardware resource binds. A compute-bound matrix multiplication speeds up only when the hardware provides a higher-throughput datapath for the narrower format, whereas a bandwidth-bound decode speeds up in proportion to the bytes removed from each token’s traffic. The worked examples below bracket the payoff, first on the reference H100 for a compute-bound layer and then on the running case’s phone for GPT-2 XL decoding.

Napkin Math 10.2: The quantization speedup (compute bound)
Problem: A compute-bound matrix multiplication (for example, in a transformer multilayer perceptron block at a large batch) switches from FP16 to INT8 on the reference H100. What throughput speedup can the integer matrix units deliver?

Math: On hardware with dedicated INT8 units:

  1. Integer throughput path: The H100 rates its INT8 matrix path at 1979 TOPS against 989 TFLOP/s on the dense FP16 path (NVIDIA Corporation 2022; Choquette 2023). The peak throughput increase is that spec ratio, about 2×.
  2. Memory bandwidth: Packed INT8 weights carry half the raw weight bytes of FP16. Their streaming time can approach half at the same achieved bandwidth, before metadata and conversion costs.
  3. Combined effect: For compute-bound operations, the ideal throughput factor is about 2× at comparable utilization of the two arithmetic paths.

Systems insight: The speedup from quantization depends on the bottleneck. Compute-bound operations (large batch sizes, high arithmetic intensity \(\ArithmeticIntensity\)) can approach about 2× at comparable utilization of supported INT8 units, where the gain comes from the integer matrix path rather than reduced memory traffic. The bandwidth-bound case inverts this. Halving the bytes moved (FP16 to INT8) yields up to 2×, and larger bit-width reductions scale the gain further, as the decode example below traces.

Choquette, Jack. 2023. “NVIDIA Hopper H100 GPU: Scaling Performance.” IEEE Micro 43 (3): 9–17. https://doi.org/10.1109/mm.2023.3256796.
NVIDIA Corporation. 2022. NVIDIA H100 Tensor Core GPU Architecture. NVIDIA Corporation.

Processors without dedicated matrix units see the same compute-bound effect through data-level parallelism in their registers, the path a phone’s CPU takes before a model moves to its integer accelerator. Fixed-width vector execution units (SIMD) and systolic arrays process more operands per cycle when each operand occupies fewer bits.

Napkin Math 10.3: The SIMD multiplier
Problem: A compute-bound layer executes using INT8 rather than FP32 on the same processor. Determine the architectural source of arithmetic speedup under fixed register width.

Mechanism: SIMD execution. A CPU or GPU core processes data in fixed-width vector registers (for example, AVX-512 is 512 bits wide).

Math:

  1. Register width: 512 bits.
  2. FP32 capacity: 512/32 = 16 elements per vector instruction.
  3. INT8 capacity: 512/8 = 64 elements per vector instruction.

Result: Switching to INT8 packs 4× more elements into the same register. \(\text{Throughput Gain} = \text{INT8 elements/inst} / \text{FP32 elements/inst}\) = 64/16 = 4×

Systems insight: The packing ratio gives an ideal 4× throughput factor, assuming comparable vector-instruction throughput, occupied lanes, and supported operators. It is a candidate bound for compute-bound layers, not a measured layer speedup; memory traffic and conversion overhead still affect execution.

When an operation is bandwidth bound, speedup tracks the reduction in bytes transferred per token rather than peak arithmetic throughput. In autoregressive token generation with small batch sizes, memory bus traffic dominates latency.

Napkin Math 10.4: The quantization speedup (bandwidth bound)
Problem: The running case’s phone decodes GPT-2 XL (1.5B parameters) at batch size one with a full 1,024-token context. Its memory bus delivers 51.2 GB/s, and each token must read every weight and the whole KV cache. How much faster does decoding run with INT4 weights than with FP16 weights?

Math (FP16 weights):

  1. Weights: 3 GB at two bytes per parameter.
  2. KV cache: Keys and values across 48 layers, 25 heads, and 64 dimensions per head, stored in FP16 for 1,024 tokens, occupy 0.31 GB.
  3. Total per token: 3.3 GB, which fits the phone’s 8 GB but takes 65 ms to stream, or about 15 tokens/s.

Fix (INT4 weights):

  1. Weights: 0.75 GB at half a byte per parameter, for 1.06 GB per token with the unchanged FP16 KV cache.
  2. Speed: Streaming that total takes 21 ms, or about 48 tokens/s.

Systems insight: INT4 weights yield a 3.1× speedup in this traffic model, below the 4\(\times\) weight reduction because the FP16 KV-cache traffic is unchanged. The model already fit in memory at FP16; quantization bought speed, not feasibility. The realized speedup still depends on a kernel that unpacks INT4 weights inside the matrix multiplication rather than in a separate pass.

Across three vision networks (Inception v3, MobileNet v1, and ResNet v2), INT8 conversion contracts weight storage by exactly \(4\times\) (135 MB to 33.75 MB for the illustrative Inception bar), while the latency change varies with the architecture and its kernels (figure 27).

Quantization speedup depends on whether memory bandwidth or compute throughput is binding.

18 Activation-aware weight quantization (AWQ): Salience is determined by activation magnitude, not weight magnitude, a distinction that matters because a small weight multiplied by a large activation produces a large output contribution. AWQ protects about 1 percent of salient weight channels through activation-aware scaling while quantizing most weights to low-bit formats, so GPT-2 XL’s 3 GB of FP16 weights can move toward an INT4-class footprint of about 0.75 GB before metadata, scales, and kernel-specific packing overheads (Lin et al. 2024).

Lin, Ji, Jiaming Tang, Haotian Tang, Shang Yang, Wei-Ming Chen, Wei-Chen Wang, Guangxuan Xiao, Xingyu Dang, Chuang Gan, and Song Han. 2024. “AWQ: Activation-Aware Weight Quantization for on-Device LLM Compression and Acceleration.” Proceedings of Machine Learning and Systems 6: 87–100.

Activation-aware methods such as activation-aware weight quantization (AWQ)18 target weight traffic in large language model (LLM) inference. This approach serves exactly the regime of GPT-2 XL decoding on the phone, where loading model weights limits the token rate. Storing the most sensitive 1 percent of weights in FP16 while quantizing the remaining 99 percent to INT4 would preserve accuracy, but mixing bit widths inside a weight matrix breaks contiguous memory access and slows down vector kernels. Instead of using mixed precision, AWQ multiplies each salient weight channel, identified by the average magnitude of the input activation channel it multiplies, by a scale factor greater than one before quantizing all weights uniformly to INT4, and divides the corresponding input activation channel by the same factor so the mathematical product is unchanged. Scaling a salient channel up before rounding and scaling it back down afterward shrinks its relative quantization error while keeping every weight in a uniform, hardware-friendly INT4 layout. Packed low-bit weights can then reduce the memory traffic required during token generation when the runtime provides a matching kernel (Lin et al. 2024).

Checkpoint 10.3: Quantization and precision checkpoint

Quantization design review criteria:

These speedups are bounds computed from bytes and peak rates. Whether a deployed model reaches them depends on how its compressed operations are lowered onto kernels, which is the third tier of the stack.

Self-Check: Question

  1. According to the chapter’s Horowitz energy constants, an INT8 integer addition consumes roughly \(0.03\text{ pJ}\) compared to \(0.90\text{ pJ}\) for an FP32 addition—a \(30\times\) energy reduction despite only a \(4\times\) reduction in bit-width. What explains this operation-level energy dividend?

    1. INT8 arithmetic eliminates the need for registers and ALU logic on the silicon die
    2. INT8 quantization automatically prunes zero-valued parameters before they reach the execution units
    3. The 8-bit integer adder circuit requires significantly fewer logic gates, capacitance, and switching energy per operation than a 32-bit floating-point adder with exponent alignment and normalization logic
    4. Floating-point operations require continuous synchronization with host CPU DRAM on every instruction

  2. In affine (asymmetric) quantization, the integer parameter ____ shifts the quantized grid so that real-valued zero maps exactly to an integer representation, ensuring that zero-padded tensor regions introduce no numerical bias.

  3. Order the stages of a standard post-training quantization workflow with static activation calibration: (1) quantize the finalized weights and export the deployment artifact, (2) run representative floating-point calibration inputs through the model and collect activation statistics, (3) choose activation clipping bounds from the collected statistics, (4) derive activation scales and zero-points from those chosen bounds.

  4. Compare two validation settings for low-bit quantization: (1) candidate INT4 weight-only quantization for autoregressive decoding when profiling shows weight traffic dominates, and (2) candidate low-bit quantization for large-batch training of the same model. Explain why these two settings require different validation conditions before concluding the quantization is worthwhile.

  5. During post-training quantization of a convolutional network, activation profiling reveals that values are heavily concentrated near zero with a small set of extreme positive outliers. Which calibration strategy best preserves numerical resolution for the bulk of typical activations?

    1. Max-absolute-value calibration, because extending the quantization grid to include extreme outliers guarantees zero clipping error across all layers
    2. Uncalibrated uniform quantization, because activation distributions in neural networks always follow a perfectly uniform probability density
    3. Static symmetric quantization with range [-128, +127] mapped unconditionally to [-1.0, +1.0] across every layer
    4. Percentile or KL-divergence (entropy) calibration, which can clip extreme tails so more integer levels represent the dense bulk of activations

  6. A team quantizing a deep convolutional network finds that per-channel (filter-wise) quantization achieves significantly higher accuracy than per-tensor (layer-wise) quantization at the same INT8 bit-width. Which mechanism explains this accuracy advantage?

    1. Per-channel quantization eliminates the need to compute or store scale factors and zero-points
    2. Individual convolutional filters within a layer often exhibit drastically different weight magnitude ranges; per-channel quantization assigns an independent scale factor to each filter, preventing wide-range filters from degrading the precision of narrow-range filters
    3. Per-tensor quantization can only be executed on CPUs, whereas per-channel quantization is restricted to edge microcontrollers
    4. Per-channel quantization automatically converts float operations into sparse matrix multiplications

See Answers →

10.4 Hardware Implementation: Changing How the Model Executes

Pruning a model structurally to half its weights and quantizing the survivors from FP32 to INT8 cuts stored weight bytes by 2× and 4×, or 8× in all. Yet in production such a model can easily run only 1.5× faster, roughly 18.8 percent of the paper projection. Profiling reveals where the missing speedup vanishes: sparse tensors continue to stream through dense kernels that cannot skip unindexed zeros, reduced-precision operators trigger unfused type conversions that flood the memory bus, and small layers spend more time launching kernels and moving intermediates than doing arithmetic. Paper metrics (parameter counts, theoretical FLOPs, and nominal bit depths) establish upper bounds that hold only when a workload is limited purely by weight traffic and every kernel exploits the new representation. Bridging the divide between paper reduction and measured hardware speedup is the job of the third tier of the stack, efficient hardware implementation.

The measured bottleneck selects the implementation technique. Kernel fusion consolidates memory-bound operator chains, custom dequantization loops keep packed low-precision weights inside cache, and hardware sparsity engines align with tensor layouts to bypass zeroed arithmetic.

10.4.1 Compression-specific fusion

In the camera pipeline’s MobileNetV2, every inverted residual block chains a convolution, a batch normalization, and a rectified linear unit (ReLU). Executed as separate kernels, each operator writes its output to accelerator global memory and the next reads that output back, paying three memory round-trips for work that could stay in on-chip registers. Kernel fusion (or operator fusion) eliminates those redundant memory transactions by combining adjacent operators into a single kernel (Chen et al. 2018; NVIDIA 2024). Model compression introduces fusion requirements of its own. Folding batch normalization into a pruned convolution, keeping a dequantize step inside the matrix-multiplication kernel that consumes it, and choosing which adjacent operators of a rewritten graph can share one kernel are all fusion decisions that arise once the graph has been compressed.

Chen, Tianqi, Thierry Moreau, Ziheng Jiang, Lianmin Zheng, Eddie Q. Yan, Haichen Shen, Meghan Cowan, et al. 2018. “TVM: An Automated End-to-End Optimizing Compiler for Deep Learning.” Proceedings of the 13th USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation (OSDI ’18), 578–94.

That read-compute-write cycle creates memory bandwidth bottlenecks for operations with low arithmetic intensity (FLOP/byte). The traffic rule for a fused chain, established in The memory wall and kernel fusion, bounds what fusion can recover. Fusing a chain of \(\ChainedOperationCount\) operations over an intermediate tensor of \(\IntermediateTensorSize\) bytes cuts idealized read-and-write traffic from \(2\,\ChainedOperationCount \IntermediateTensorSize\) to \(2\IntermediateTensorSize\) bytes, because the fused kernel reads its input once and writes its final output once. Weights, auxiliary inputs, cache effects, and register spills are outside this simple bound, and data dependencies, tensor shapes, and register pressure limit how much can legally be fused. Several inference patterns nevertheless approximate the bound closely enough for fusion to matter.

Convolution-BatchNorm-ReLU fusion

The common Conv-BN-ReLU pattern can replace three launches and intermediate round-trips with one fused path when the compiler can legally fold batch normalization and combine the activation (listing 4). Folding is compression-specific because it must happen after the convolution’s channels and scales are final. During training, batch normalization recomputes channel means and variances across every mini-batch, but at inference time those statistics freeze into fixed per-channel constants: the running mean \(\BatchNormRunningMean\) and the stabilized running standard deviation \(\BatchNormRunningStandardDeviation\) (the square root of the sum of running variance and the numerical stabilizer). Once frozen, batch normalization is a fixed per-channel affine rescaling that subtracts \(\BatchNormRunningMean\), divides by \(\BatchNormRunningStandardDeviation\), multiplies by the learned scale \(\NormalizationScaleVector\), and adds the learned shift \(\NormalizationShiftVector\) directly to the linear output of the convolution. Because composing two linear transformations yields a single linear transformation, distributing \(\frac{\NormalizationScaleVector}{\BatchNormRunningStandardDeviation}\) across the convolution output folds batch normalization into the preceding convolution weights (\(\FusedConvolutionWeight= \frac{\NormalizationScaleVector}{\BatchNormRunningStandardDeviation} \WeightMatrix\)) and adjusts the convolution bias to \(\FusedConvolutionBias = \frac{\NormalizationScaleVector}{\BatchNormRunningStandardDeviation}(\BiasVector- \BatchNormRunningMean) + \NormalizationShiftVector\). Batch normalization’s per-channel scale thus multiplies into the surviving filters, and its shift becomes the convolution’s bias, so a pruned or quantized network is folded once its structure stops changing.

The idealized intermediate stream falls from six transfers to two.

The intended function is preserved, although batch-normalization folding changes the arithmetic sequence, and the intermediate traffic falls by 3×. Generic bias-activation and attention fusion, by contrast, do not depend on compression and belong to the general fusion toolkit of ML Frameworks.

↩︎ IO-aware attention tiling and its HBM-traffic accounting are established in FlashAttention: IO-aware attention optimization.

A ResNet-50 layer with 256 channels and a \(28{\times}28\) spatial size puts numbers on this. Its feature map occupies 802.8 KB, so the four intermediate transfers that fusion removes amount to \(4 \times 256 \times 28 \times 28 \times 4 \text{ bytes} \approx \text{3.2 MB}\) per layer. The full accounting also counts weights and parameters. Without fusion, each operation performs its own memory round-trip: Conv reads input (802.8 KB) plus weights (2.4 MB) and writes output (802.8 KB), totaling 4 MB. BN then reads that output, adds its parameters (2 KB), and writes again, for 1.6 MB. ReLU repeats the pattern for another 1.6 MB. The total unfused memory traffic is 7.2 MB. With fusion, the entire sequence reads input and weights once and writes the final output once, requiring only 4 MB, a 44.8 percent bandwidth reduction.

At the reference H100’s 3,350 GB/s HBM bandwidth, these traffic volumes imply ideal lower bounds of 2 microseconds before fusion and 1.2 microseconds after fusion, a 1.81× ratio for the traffic component alone. These are not full layer latencies because convolution compute, cache effects, and launch overhead must also be included. Furthermore, fusion needs the computation captured as a graph, and dynamic control flow, in-place mutation, or unsupported operations can block the rewrite, so the exported plan must be inspected rather than assumed (graph capture is developed in Graph capture and JIT compilation).

↪ The register pressure and accelerator-specific constraints that bound how far kernels can be fused on GPUs and TPUs are developed in Performance benefits and constraints.

Quantization adds a fusion of its own. A weight-only INT8 or INT4 kernel must turn each packed integer code \(\QuantizedIntegerCode\) back into a real value \(\QuantizerInput\) before multiplying it, using the scale \(\QuantizationScale\) and zero-point \(\QuantizationZeroPoint\) of equation 1 and equation 2: \[ \QuantizerInput= \QuantizationScale(\QuantizedIntegerCode - \QuantizationZeroPoint) \tag{4}\] Executed as a separate kernel, dequantization writes the full-precision weights back to memory and reads them again, restoring the traffic that quantization removed. Fused into the matrix-multiplication kernel, the dequantize step runs in registers on each tile as it arrives, so only the packed bytes cross the memory bus. That fusion is what lets GPT-2 XL’s INT4 weights deliver the decode speedup computed in section 10.3.5. When a runtime cannot fuse it, the quantized model can run slower than its FP16 baseline, a failure section 10.5.4.2 examines. Fusion removes intermediate traffic between operations that must all run, leaving the complementary hardware question of how to skip operands that pruning has already set to zero.

10.4.2 Sparsity exploitation

Replacing pruned weights with zeros inside a standard dense matrix still fetches every zero from memory and multiplies it in the ALU, yielding no wall-clock speedup. Sparsity exploitation designs the storage layouts and kernels that translate sparsity,19 where a substantial fraction of tensor entries are zero, into reduced memory traffic and execution time.

19 Sparsity: From Latin sparsus (scattered), past participle of spargere (to scatter); in ML, \(\ell_1\) regularization such as the lasso can induce exact zeros rather than merely small values (Tibshirani 1996). Software can represent arbitrary sparsity patterns, while efficient hardware execution generally requires a supported format or structured pattern (for example, NVIDIA’s 2:4 sparsity). The gap between representable and executable sparsity separates theoretical compression from realized speedup.

Tibshirani, Robert. 1996. “Regression Shrinkage and Selection via the Lasso.” Journal of the Royal Statistical Society: Series B (Methodological) 58 (1): 267–88. https://doi.org/10.1111/j.2517-6161.1996.tb02080.x.

Recall from section 10.2.1.2 that pruning a weight matrix \(\SparseWeightMatrix\in \mathbb{R}^{\WeightMatrixRowCount\times \WeightMatrixColumnCount}\) removes a fraction \(\SparsityRatio\) of its entries. From the hardware’s perspective, that nominal sparsity ratio counts the exact zeros in the stored tensor: \[ \SparsityRatio= \frac{\Vert \ZeroEntryIndicator\Vert_0}{\WeightMatrixRowCount\times \WeightMatrixColumnCount} \] where \(\ZeroEntryIndicator\) yields 1 if entry \((\MatrixRowIndex,\MatrixColumnIndex)\) is zero and 0 otherwise, and \(\Vert \cdot \Vert_0\) counts nonzero entries. When magnitude thresholding at \(\PruningThreshold\) zeroes out every entry whose magnitude falls below \(\PruningThreshold\), the resulting thresholded sparsity ratio is: \[ \ThresholdedSparsityRatio= \frac{\Vert \BelowThresholdIndicator \Vert_0}{\WeightMatrixRowCount\times \WeightMatrixColumnCount} \] Whether those zeros come from exact masking or thresholding at \(\PruningThreshold\), they yield wall-clock acceleration only when the execution pipeline skips both the arithmetic and the associated memory traffic.

From an execution standpoint, the spatial pattern of zeros determines memory transaction efficiency. Unstructured masks zero arbitrary scalar coordinates, requiring pointer arrays and non-unit-stride gathers that degrade bandwidth on SIMD and systolic units. Conversely, structured patterns, such as channel pruning or the 2:4 N:M constraint of section 10.2.1.3, preserve predictable memory layouts that align with standard dense kernels or dedicated sparse datapaths.

Sparsity utilization methods

Translating zeros into runtime speedup requires a representation that encodes nonzero positions and a kernel that decodes those coordinates to bypass unneeded computation (Hoefler et al. 2021). In a matrix-vector product, a dense \(4{\times}4\) operator executes 16 multiply-accumulate operations, whereas a sparse execution path computes only the six nonzero products alongside the necessary metadata lookups: \[ \begin{bmatrix} 2 & 0 & 0 & 1 \\ 0 & 3 & 0 & 0 \\ 4 & 0 & 5 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & 6 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} \SparseProductInputComponent_1 \\ \SparseProductInputComponent_2 \\ \SparseProductInputComponent_3 \\ \SparseProductInputComponent_4 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 2\SparseProductInputComponent_1 + \SparseProductInputComponent_4 \\ 3\SparseProductInputComponent_2 \\ 4\SparseProductInputComponent_1 + 5\SparseProductInputComponent_3 \\ 6\SparseProductInputComponent_4 \end{bmatrix} \]

The runtime benefit depends entirely on whether the latency eliminated by skipping 10 multiply-accumulate operations exceeds the latency added by coordinate decoding and non-contiguous activation loads. For unstructured sparsity, that trade has a computable answer in bytes and a measurable one in time.

Storage and execution break-even

A variant of the running case makes the byte side concrete. Suppose the camera team must also ship its MobileNetV2 to a wearable companion device whose microcontroller offers only 2 MB of flash memory, against the model’s 14 MB of FP32 weights. Retraining a smaller architecture from scratch would require weeks of data collection and validation that the product schedule does not allow. Suppose profiling shows that about 85.7 percent of the model’s weights are near zero and contribute little on the validation set. Removing those weights and fine-tuning the remainder for a few epochs leaves surviving weight values that total 2 MB with an acceptable accuracy loss. Whether the stored artifact also fits in 2 MB depends on how the surviving weights are stored. The numbers are a labeled scenario that anchors the engineering trade-off, not a universal MobileNet benchmark.

Storing only the surviving values requires an indexed sparse encoding such as Compressed Sparse Row (CSR) or coordinate list (COO). Because these formats must store positional metadata (such as column indices and row pointers) alongside the nonzero values, metadata overhead offsets savings at low sparsity. For example, storing a 16-bit floating-point weight with a 16-bit column index doubles the per-element byte footprint, requiring over 50 percent sparsity just to break even on artifact size (\(\DataVolume\)). Storing a dense tensor with zeroed entries saves zero memory. The wearable variant shows the consequence. Its surviving weights total 2 MB only if they cost nothing to locate. A 16-bit index beside each value adds half again to 32-bit values and doubles 16-bit ones, so the stored artifact misses the 2 MB flash budget. A denser position encoding, such as a bitmap, reduces the metadata overhead but cannot fit unchanged values that already consume the entire budget. Fitting requires further pruning or lower-precision values with room reserved for metadata, or structured removal of whole channels so that the remaining tensors can be stored without per-value indices.

↪ The memory footprint and index-pointer layouts for compressed sparse row (CSR) representations are collected for reference in Sparse matrix formats.

Nor does unstructured sparsity translate directly to inference acceleration on modern hardware. Commodity accelerators achieve peak throughput (\(\PeakRate\)) through dense systolic arrays and wide SIMD execution units that rely on memory coalescing. Memory transfers use device-dependent transaction granularity, and caches store data in cache lines. Coalescing nearby, aligned accesses lets parallel threads share transactions more efficiently. A dense masked tensor still moves its zero entries. An indexed sparse encoding instead packs nonzero values, but Sparse Matrix-Dense Matrix Multiplication (SpMM) kernels must decode metadata and gather the matching activations. Irregular activation accesses, load imbalance, and kernel overhead can offset the skipped arithmetic even when stored weight values are contiguous. The performance break-even point depends on shape, format, batch size, and the available sparse kernel, so artifact reduction alone does not establish a speedup.

Structured patterns

Structured patterns resolve the break-even problem by constraining where zeros occur, and achieving actual speedups from them requires hardware that can efficiently skip zero-valued computations. Processor architectures differ in which patterns they exploit. Ampere-generation and later Sparse Tensor Cores exploit fine-grained 2:4 structured patterns, while systolic arrays require dense block structures. Libraries such as cuSPARSE reformulate sparse computations into patterns the hardware handles well, and MegaBlocks (Gale et al. 2022) recasts sparse mixture-of-experts training as block-sparse operations on dense tiles.

Gale, Trevor, Deepak Narayanan, Cliff Young, and Matei Zaharia. 2022. “MegaBlocks: Efficient Sparse Training with Mixture-of-Experts.” arXiv Preprint.

A sparse pattern earns hardware speedup only when it is regular enough for kernels to predict. Two formats make that regularity explicit: block sparse matrices and N:M sparsity patterns. Block sparse matrices group nonzeros into dense submatrices, so operations on the large sparse matrix become a smaller number of dense operations on submatrices. In the illustrated representation, \(3\times 3\) dense sub-blocks are indexed by an auxiliary coordinate array, so sub-block kernels can bypass the empty tiles (figure 28) (NVIDIA 2020). The block size is a teaching choice. Actual speedup requires a block shape, layout, datatype, and kernel supported by the target library and hardware; regular blocks alone do not guarantee aligned memory transactions or Tensor Core execution.

Similarly, N:M sparsity retains at most \(\StructuredSparsityNonzeroCount\) nonzeros in each group of \(\StructuredSparsityGroupSize\) consecutive elements; hardware-oriented pruning often enforces exactly \(\StructuredSparsityNonzeroCount\) retained values so every group has the same representation (Zhou et al. 2021). The deterministic format lets a supported kernel predict the value and metadata layout while retaining more capacity than removing whole channels or blocks. That regularity creates an executable compromise between arbitrary masks and dense computation, but only for targets with the matching path.

Zhou, Aojun, Yukun Ma, Junnan Zhu, Jianbo Liu, Zhijie Zhang, Kun Yuan, Wenxiu Sun, and Hongsheng Li. 2021. “Learning n:m Fine-Grained Structured Sparse Neural Networks from Scratch.” arXiv Preprint.
Lu, Yucheng, Shivani Agrawal, Suvinay Subramanian, Oleg Rybakov, Christopher De Sa, and Amir Yazdanbakhsh. 2023. “STEP: Learning n:m Structured Sparsity Masks from Scratch with Precondition.” arXiv Preprint.

On hardware with a sparse matrix path, the 2:4 pattern of section 10.2.1.3 turns into execution savings, since its 2-bit position indices cost far less than the pointer arrays of general CSR or COO formats (NVIDIA Corporation 2020). The sparse datapath that consumes those indices and skips the zeroed multiplies is developed in N:M structured sparsity mechanics. Learned masks need not stop at 2:4; STEP, for example, learns more general N:M masks for inference (Lu et al. 2023).

Unstructured pruning zeroes arbitrary individual weights. Those irregular locations can disrupt memory coalescing and lane utilization, while Compressed Sparse Row (CSR) or Coordinate (COO) metadata adds storage and non-unit-stride accesses. An unstructured sparse model can therefore run slower than its dense counterpart when metadata and underutilization outweigh skipped arithmetic. TPUs are useful contrast cases for dense systolic-array acceleration (Jouppi et al. 2021), but sparse acceleration still depends on a specific hardware and software path rather than accepting arbitrary masks. Field-programmable gate arrays can implement application-specific sparse formats, yet their efficiency depends on the chosen dataflow and resource budget; programmability is not a universal sparse-speedup guarantee.

Jouppi, Norman P., Doe Hyun Yoon, Matthew Ashcraft, Mark Gottscho, Thomas B. Jablin, George Kurian, James Laudon, et al. 2021. “Ten Lessons from Three Generations Shaped Google’s TPUv4i: Industrial Product.” 2021 ACM/IEEE 48th Annual International Symposium on Computer Architecture (ISCA), 1–14. https://doi.org/10.1109/isca52012.2021.00010.
Gale, Trevor, Matei Zaharia, Cliff Young, and Erich Elsen. 2020. “Sparse GPU Kernels for Deep Learning.” SC20: International Conference for High Performance Computing, Networking, Storage and Analysis 2020: 8955–67. https://doi.org/10.1109/sc41405.2020.00021.

Across all platforms, sparse operations can reduce memory bandwidth requirements and energy consumption when the sparse representation and kernels actually skip data movement rather than only arithmetic. This benefit compounds with quantization, since a sparse INT8 model can require less memory traffic than either technique alone when the format overhead is small enough (Hoefler et al. 2021; Gale et al. 2020).

Challenges and limitations

Unstructured sparsity pays off only after crossing a hardware- and kernel-specific break-even point.

The dependence of every sparse speedup on a matching format and kernel explains why nominal sparsity often fails to deliver speedups in practice. First, the performance break-even point varies with tensor dimensions, sparse format, kernel implementation, and hardware architecture. When coordinate metadata and irregular activation gathers saturate cache lines, an unstructured sparse kernel can run slower than an optimized dense baseline until sparsity exceeds 80 to 90 percent. Second, the accuracy-efficiency trade-off is nonlinear, because models often tolerate moderate pruning before task quality degrades abruptly past a critical density threshold. Finally, energy savings do not scale linearly with skipped operations; irregular memory access and index decoding can consume more power on edge devices than the arithmetic they eliminate. Fusion and sparse kernels thus decide whether each technique’s paper savings survive contact with the hardware. Most deployments apply several techniques at once, and choosing and ordering them is the next decision.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A paper estimate predicts an \(8\times\) speedup for a compressed model under ideal assumptions, but the implementation measured on an unmodified GPU delivers only \(1.5\times\). Which statement best defines the role of hardware implementation in explaining this gap?

    1. It aligns computation graphs, memory access layouts, operator scheduling, and sparsity patterns with physical accelerator architectures so that theoretical compression can translate into measured wall-clock speedup
    2. It reduces the volume of training data needed to fine-tune compressed neural networks
    3. It eliminates all memory-bound operations by converting every neural network layer into a compute-bound GEMM
    4. It automates the hyperparameter tuning of learning rates and batch sizes during pretraining

  2. Operator fusion of Conv-BatchNorm-ReLU sequences produces substantial execution speedup on modern GPUs even though the fused kernel preserves the intended layer function. Which mechanism explains this latency reduction?

    1. Fusion reduces the total number of weight parameters stored in the convolutional layer
    2. Fusion retrains the network to use lower numerical precision during the forward pass
    3. Fusion skips zero-valued activation elements by converting dense tensors to sparse matrices
    4. Fusion executes convolution, batch normalization, and ReLU inside a single GPU kernel, keeping intermediate activations on chip and cutting intermediate-tensor traffic and launch overhead

  3. A compressed neural network achieves a \(50\%\) reduction in total floating-point operations (FLOPs), yet on the deployment accelerator, end-to-end inference latency decreases by only \(10\%\). Explain two distinct hardware and architectural mechanisms that cause this discrepancy.

  4. True or False: Commodity SIMD vector units automatically achieve proportional latency reductions on weight matrices with \(50\%\) unstructured sparsity because vector lanes automatically skip zero values without overhead.

  5. A team optimizes a deep network for NVIDIA Ampere GPUs that feature hardware-accelerated 2:4 structured sparsity. Which compression strategy directly engages this dedicated accelerator capability?

    1. Unconstrained unstructured magnitude pruning, because maximum zero count always yields the highest speedup on Ampere
    2. Dynamic channel pruning that alters tensor shapes per batch, because Tensor Cores require dynamic input dimensions
    3. Structured 2:4 sparsity (exactly 2 non-zero values in every contiguous block of 4 elements), because Ampere Tensor Cores include a supported sparse path for that pattern
    4. Activation checkpointing, because recomputing intermediate activations during inference eliminates sparse matrix indexing overhead

See Answers →

10.5 Choosing and Combining Techniques

When the chapter’s phone decodes GPT-2 XL, its FP16 weights and KV cache claim 3.3 GB of the 8 GB that the operating system and other applications also share. Each generated token waits about 65 ms while every weight streams over the 51.2 GB/s bus, and the handset sustains only 3–5 W before thermal throttling. Choosing among compression techniques, and ordering them, starts from whichever of those physical limits binds first, alongside the allowable task accuracy loss and the available training budget.

The three primary optimization levers target different levels of the system stack. Distillation alters the algorithmic architecture by training a compact dense student to emulate a larger teacher, executing efficiently on standard hardware without specialized runtime support. Pruning modifies tensor topology and operation patterns, but yields wall-clock acceleration only when the resulting sparsity aligns with hardware execution units, such as 2:4 structured sparse matrix cores or channel-aligned sub-matrices. Quantization alters data representation by reducing numerical bit width, directly lowering memory bus traffic and cache footprint while engaging higher-throughput integer datapaths. Their first-order trade-offs, with representative ranges for common choices, sit side by side in table 11.

10.5.1 The accuracy-efficiency frontier

Pushing a single compression knob too far, such as pruning 90 percent of a network’s weights or quantizing sensitive layers to 4 bits without retraining, crosses a cliff where a modest extra byte saving costs 5 to 15 percentage points of task accuracy (table 11). Every lever in the table moves the model along this accuracy-efficiency trade-off: additional model capacity can improve predictive accuracy while demanding larger weight payloads, expanded activation working sets, and higher memory bandwidth during inference. On hardware with strict memory or power limits, engineers first eliminate avoidable implementation costs, then choose among feasible operating points on the Pareto frontier (principle 5).

Systems Perspective 10.2: The compression-accuracy trade-off curve
The measured trade-off is not uniform. A structure-preserving implementation improvement can move a dominated model toward the frontier with little or no accuracy loss. Once on an efficient frontier, improving one objective trades against another; aggressive structural changes can enter regions with steep quality penalties.

The engineering decision is where to stop. The “knee” of the curve marks a region where further resource savings incur a sharper quality penalty. It is a useful visual diagnostic, but accuracy points cannot be compared directly with milliseconds, bytes, or joules. Stop at an operating point that meets the deployment’s quality and resource thresholds, using explicit objective weights if a further trade must be ranked.

Techniques that preserve model structure, such as kernel fusion and modest precision reduction (FP32 to BF16), can yield latency or memory gains with little quality loss when the model and execution path support them. In contrast, techniques that alter network structure, such as pruning and distillation, yield larger reductions in memory and arithmetic volume but risk significant quality degradation without careful retraining. Optimization therefore begins by identifying the binding hardware constraint, such as memory capacity on mobile devices, frame latency in interactive pipelines, or energy per inference on battery-powered sensors. Each tier can then target the resource that actually binds. Structural methods cut parameter and operation counts, quantization shrinks memory traffic and raises arithmetic intensity, and compiler-level fusion eliminates intermediate memory round-trips. The regions in table 11 mark where each choice typically lands on that curve, and a structured decision framework maps each binding hardware constraint to the specific levers worth testing first.

Checkpoint 10.4: The efficiency frontier

Optimization can remove avoidable costs or trade among efficient operating points.

Trade-offs

10.5.2 Decision framework

Applying a compression technique that targets the wrong hardware bottleneck wastes engineering cycles and task accuracy. Pruning FLOPs from a layer stalled on off-chip weight streaming leaves memory-bus latency intact, whereas quantizing weights to 8 bits on a compute-bound processor that lacks an integer matrix unit leaves arithmetic throughput unchanged. Identifying the binding hardware constraint first narrows the search to the optimization tiers that can relieve it. Under the D·A·M taxonomy, compression methods redistribute resource demands across Algorithm (topology, pruning, distillation, and the numerical representation of weights) and Machine (execution units and memory hierarchies); range-calibration sets are the main Data-axis input. Each system constraint maps to the optimization tiers that can relieve it (table 12), where the marks identify mechanisms to investigate rather than guaranteed deployment gains.

One technique can move more than one term of the iron law. For example, quantizing weights from FP32 to INT8 directly relieves on-device storage capacity. A bandwidth-limited operator approaches proportional latency savings only when the affected traffic dominates and conversion overhead does not consume the gain. A compute-limited operator needs a faster supported execution path. Unstructured pruning likewise reduces executed arithmetic only when sparse kernels exploit the removed weights; indexing and irregular memory accesses can offset the saving. The changed representation therefore supplies a candidate improvement whose effect must be measured on the target.

When persistent storage or device memory capacity binds the deployment, as over-the-air package limits and microcontroller flash budgets do, quantization provides the most direct payload reduction. Relative to FP32 storage, INT8 post-training quantization (PTQ) cuts raw weight storage by 4\(\times\) without requiring retraining cycles. INT4 cuts raw payload by 8\(\times\), though aggressive bit-width reduction increases the risk of accuracy degradation. If uniform low-bit quantization causes unacceptable accuracy loss on sensitive layers, distilling knowledge into a smaller dense architecture followed by moderate quantization preserves fidelity while satisfying the storage budget.

When latency is compute-bound, the workload operates above the accelerator’s ridge point, the arithmetic intensity at which its compute and memory-bandwidth limits meet, saturating execution units. Relieving this bottleneck requires reducing executed operations or increasing arithmetic throughput. Structured pruning removes channels, attention heads, or entire layers, yielding smaller dense matrices that execute on standard libraries without sparse runtime overhead. However, pruning to dimensions that misalign with hardware warp or tile sizes (such as channel counts not divisible by 8 or 16) degrades tensor core efficiency and curtails wall-clock gains. If the target platform provides dedicated integer execution units, INT8 quantization increases the instruction throughput rate, accelerating compute-bound kernels directly. Early-exit architectures provide an alternative algorithmic path by dynamically halting inference on simpler inputs, provided that routing and pipeline synchronization overhead do not disrupt batch processing.

↪ How the ridge point shifts across accelerators and precisions is developed in Hardware ridge points.

Small-batch transformer generation often exposes a weight-bandwidth bottleneck, as GPT-2 XL decoding on the phone showed, because dense decoding streams large parameter matrices for matrix-vector operations with low arithmetic intensity. When fetched weights dominate latency, weight-only quantization (such as INT4 or INT8 weights paired with FP16 activations) can approach a speedup proportional to the reduction in those bytes. KV-cache traffic and dequantization overhead limit that gain without necessarily making execution compute-bound; larger batches can increase weight reuse and shift the limiting resource.

When energy or thermal dissipation binds, quantization provides substantial benefits because moving data across off-chip memory buses consumes orders of magnitude more energy per bit than on-chip arithmetic or local SRAM access. Reducing operand bit widths directly lowers bus capacitance charging energy, while integer ALUs consume less switching energy than floating-point datapaths. Structured pruning compounds these savings by eliminating operations and memory transactions entirely. Full-system power audits must still measure peripheral components (memory refresh, host bus communication, and static idle leakage) to ensure that kernel-level energy savings translate into extended operating life.

Selection also depends on the engineering and computational budget available. Calibrated PTQ needs only a small representative dataset, and QAT adds training cost only when PTQ misses the accuracy threshold. Knowledge distillation costs more, requiring teacher forward passes and substantial training cycles to optimize a smaller student model. Hardware-aware NAS occupies the highest cost tier, justified only when high-volume deployment amortizes the extensive compute needed to explore the architectural search space.

Applied to GPT-2 XL decoding on the phone, this framework makes INT4 weight-only quantization the first candidate, not a completed solution. It cuts the weight bytes to a quarter of FP16, but each token also streams the unchanged KV cache, and the gain holds only when dequantization is fused into the matrix multiplication (section 10.3.5). Distillation to a smaller model is another candidate if the remaining gap justifies its training cost. Each candidate must pass task-quality, latency, peak-memory, and sustained-power checks against the phone’s 3–5 W envelope.

Choosing an individual technique addresses a single binding bottleneck, but production deployment budgets frequently require compounding gains across multiple dimensions. When memory capacity, bandwidth, and compute throughput are all constrained simultaneously, optimizing a system requires composing structural alterations, precision reductions, and custom execution kernels into an integrated transformation pipeline.

10.5.3 Combining and sequencing techniques

Quantizing a network to fixed 8-bit integer ranges before pruning its channels shifts the activation distributions that those ranges were calibrated to fit, while removing floating-point precision before distillation hinders gradient recovery. Because each tier changes the weight and activation distributions or tensor shapes that the next tier sees, the order in which compression techniques are applied governs both final accuracy and hardware utilization. On the camera pipeline, the sequence follows directly from those dependencies between the tiers. Channel pruning comes first, with pruned channel counts kept on a multiple of the hardware’s tile width so the smaller layers still fill the phone’s matrix tiles. Distillation from the ResNet-50 teacher then serves as the recovery training for the pruned network, the prune-then-distill order described in section 10.2.3. INT8 quantization comes last among the model changes, because range calibration must see the weight and activation distributions of the final structure, and because INT8 is what moves the model from the phone’s CPU onto its integer accelerator. Batch-normalization folding and the remaining fusions are applied when the graph is compiled for the device. Each step is profiled on the phone before the next is taken, because each one changes the bottleneck that the next one faces. The steps also move different quantities, and most of the frame time comes off at the step that changes the processor (figure 29).

A modeled BERT-Base pipeline attaches numbers to the same staging. Pruning, distillation, and quantization together reduce its footprint from 440 MB to 28 MB (rounded), a 16× raw-footprint reduction. Each stage addresses a distinct bottleneck. Structural pruning removes redundant matrix multiplications, distillation supervises the compact student to recover representational capacity, and quantization packs weights and activations into lower-bit integers to relieve memory bandwidth pressure.

Example 10.2: BERT-Base mobile deployment pipeline
Scenario: A modeled deployment targets executing a 110M-parameter BERT-Base language model on resource-constrained mobile hardware. The compact graph is stipulated for this example; the figures are not reported DistilBERT results.

The FP32 teacher begins at 440 MB with assigned task accuracy 76.2 percent. Structural pruning produces a stipulated 27.5M-parameter graph, a 75 percent parameter reduction, with raw FP32 weights of 110 MB and assigned accuracy 75.1 percent.

That pruned graph is the distillation student. Teacher supervision recovers its assigned accuracy to 75.9 percent without another parameter-count reduction. Final INT8 quantization leaves the same parameter count, reduces raw weight storage to 27.5 MB, and has assigned accuracy 75.6 percent. The headline ratio uses this unrounded weight payload; scales, zero-points, packing, activations, and runtime workspace remain outside the calculation.

Diagnosis: In this modeled comparison, quantization before pruning has an assigned accuracy loss of 2.1 percent. One mechanism to investigate is whether structural changes invalidate previously calibrated ranges. Pruning and distillation before quantization allow calibration to use the resulting distributions. These modeled values illustrate the mechanism and do not establish a universal ordering.

Systems lesson: In this modeled pipeline, pruning, then distillation, then post-training INT8 quantization produce a 16× footprint reduction (440 MB to 28 MB) while limiting task accuracy loss to 0.6 percent. Because tensor distributions and hardware kernel efficiencies vary across architectures, each intermediate artifact must be profiled on the target runtime.

Sequencing matters because these transformations interact through shared parameters and activation distributions. Structural pruning removes channels or attention heads, altering the dynamic range and variance of surviving weight tensors. Quantizing before pruning can leave ranges poorly matched to the resulting distributions, so recalibration or joint adaptation may be needed. In the staged candidate, floating-point recovery lets the pruned network adapt, and distillation supplies a teacher signal before final quantization. QAT itself updates floating-point master weights through approximate gradients; it does not require direct integer weight updates. Alternative schedules should be compared at matched recovery and calibration budgets.

Combining techniques also changes the size-quality trade-off itself (Han et al. 2016). In one reported setting (figure 30), while individual SVD and pruning curves suffer steep accuracy cliffs below about 15 percent and 6 percent model size respectively, combining pruning with quantization extends the zero-loss plateau down to roughly 3 percent of original parameter footprint. This comparison does not isolate transformation order. The magnitude of these gains remains model- and runtime-specific, requiring engineers to validate each transformation stage independently.

Systems Perspective 10.3: The optimization composition problem
Optimization techniques interact through shared representations and physical resources rather than composing as independent functions. Pruning changes the surviving value distribution, tensor shapes, or sparse format; quantization adds scales and changes the available kernels; fusion changes which intermediates are materialized and can alter the profile that justified earlier choices. Composing pruning with quantization, as when 2:4 structured sparsity is paired with INT8 weights, leaves an executable that must carry both the sparsity metadata and the quantization scales. A pruned shape that breaks the memory alignment a quantized kernel expects can erase the speedup of both (Hoefler et al. 2021). Effective optimization therefore treats the model-runtime-hardware combination as a coupled system, measures it after every transformation, and revisits the sequence when a new bottleneck appears. This makes compression a systems engineering problem as well as a machine learning problem.

Hoefler, Torsten, Dan Alistarh, Tal Ben-Nun, Nikoli Dryden, and Alexandra Peste. 2021. “Sparsity in Deep Learning: Pruning and Growth for Efficient Inference and Training in Neural Networks.” Journal of Machine Learning Research 22 (241): 1–124.

A footprint reduction that compounds on paper therefore becomes a speedup only where the runtime supports every transformation in the sequence at once, which makes the toolchain that applies them part of the decision.

10.5.4 Compression toolchains

Quantizing a large model by hand would mean editing thousands of operator call sites to insert scale factors and track activation ranges. Manual graph surgery also cannot guarantee autograd consistency during retraining or the memory alignment that accelerator kernels require. Compression therefore runs through tooling, and the tool choice follows the layer of the software stack that owns the transformation. Framework APIs operate on the high-level computation graph during training and calibration, where the execution engine maintains visibility into weights, activations, and autograd state. At this stage, quantization is typically simulated, with fake-quantization nodes that clamp and round values within floating-point registers, modeling numerical precision loss while preserving differentiability.

Simulating quantization does not itself establish a physical speedup. A compiler or runtime must lower the graph into supported kernels; frameworks may integrate that stage or export to a separate deployment engine. The runtime compiler fuses scale factor arithmetic directly into matrix-multiplication compute tiles and packs sparse weights into hardware-specific metadata layouts (such as 2:4 structured sparsity formats). This lowering eliminates round-trip memory transactions to HBM that would otherwise negate the benefits of reduced precision.

Reproducibility is the first requirement of a multi-stage pipeline. Compressing a model alters its numerical representations across both the algorithm and machine layers of the D·A·M taxonomy. Unrecorded calibration inputs or mismatched rounding modes can degrade accuracy silently without raising runtime errors, so every deployed artifact must remain traceable from its source training checkpoint, through the calibration data, pruning schedule, and fusions that produced it, to its compiled hardware execution graph.

↪ Versioned artifacts and the lineage graphs that keep deployed models traceable are developed in Versioning and lineage.

Model optimization APIs and tools

At the model-development layer, compression algorithms require access to runtime state that downstream deployment compilers discard: gradient histories, optimizer state variables, and dynamic activation distributions. When post-training quantization or one-shot pruning degrades accuracy beyond acceptable operating thresholds, recovery requires retraining or iterative fine-tuning. Frameworks such as PyTorch and TensorFlow provide optimization APIs specifically to manage this graph-transformation boundary. These APIs inject quantization nodes and observers, reparameterize weight tensors with sparsity masks, and preserve calibration metadata alongside the trained parameters, which is what makes a compression recipe repeatable across many models.

Quantization-aware training demonstrates this graph-level intervention. Optimization APIs insert the fake-quantization operators of section 10.3.3.2.2 into the compute graph; these operators clamp and round in the forward pass and apply the straight-through estimator of section 10.3.3.2.1 in the backward pass. Producing a deployable low-precision artifact then takes a short sequence of graph transformations and metadata tracking (listing 5).

Pruning tools implement the mask-then-export boundary of section 10.2.1.7. A mask-based API keeps the dense weight tensor \(\WeightMatrix\) and a binary mask \(\PruningMask\) of the same shape during recovery training, computing \(\PrunedWeightMatrix= \PruningMask\odot \WeightMatrix\) in the forward pass so the existing dense kernels and gradient updates keep working. An export pass then finalizes the transformation, either slicing away pruned channels or encoding the nonzeros in a sparse format the target’s kernels accept. What must be recorded along the way, so that the compressed model can be reproduced, is short but not optional (listing 6).

Hardware-specific optimization libraries

Exporting an optimized model from a training framework produces an intermediate graph representation, not an executable binary.
